Energy and AI: Sustainable, Sovereign Industrial Campuses | Alain Wilmouth, 2CRSi | RAISE 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·20:57·255 views·2026-07-23
Alain WilmouthPresident & CEO, 2CRSi Ethan PierseFounder, Borderless Ventures
TL;DR*"The reality is if you imagine tomorrow that the AI will replace hundreds or millions of job everywhere in the worlds, you have to understand that if the AI is eating all the power available in countries, what will do the rest of the humanity? ...
2CRSi CEO Alain Wilmouth and host Ethan Pierse discuss building sustainable, sovereign industrial AI infrastructure across Europe, detailing a new French consortium ("Aether") that combines immersion cooling, biomass energy, and unused agricultural land to power edge AI...
  • TL;DR: 2CRSi CEO Alain Wilmouth and host Ethan Pierse discuss building sustainable, sovereign industrial AI infrastructure across Europe, detailing a new French consortium ("Aether") that combines immersion cooling, biomass energy, and unused agricultural land to power edge AI data centers while generating negative carbon emissions.

The Energy & Cooling Challenge in AI Infrastructure

  • Early Pioneer in Liquid Cooling: Alain Wilmouth noted that 2CRSi began building servers specifically for immersion cooling and Direct Liquid Cooling (DLC) in 2013 and 2015, initially supplying US universities and national labs.
  • The Cooling Energy Burden: Historically, removing 1,000 watts of heat required spending an additional 1,000 watts on cooling.
  • Regulatory Pressures: Strict European regulations now enforce low Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) targets, such as ~1.3 in Germany and ~1.2 in the Netherlands.

The Aether Consortium and Sustainable Power

  • Consortium Launch: Announced in July 2026, "Aether" is a consortium of established industrial companies (many over 100 years old), including Dassault System, Varian, and Equans, with partners like Hafner Energy.
  • Initial Capacity: The consortium currently has 42 megawatts available across two sites near Strasbourg, France, with a roadmap scaling up to 400 megawatts.
  • Biomass and Gasification: To move beyond mere carbon neutrality to negative carbon footprints, the project integrates biomass energy using gasification technology via partner Hafner Energy, allowing carbon to be stored for centuries while burning cleaned wood gas.
  • Agricultural Land Integration: Alain Wilmouth pointed out that France has 15 million hectares of land, of which 2 million hectares have been unused by farmers for at least five years. Utilizing these lands for biomass production could create approximately 250,000 jobs for struggling farmers without requiring state grants.
  • Grid Strain Concerns: Pointing to grid constraints (such as an evening in late December when only 0.5 gigawatts of power remained available in France), Wilmouth argued against massive centralized gigawatt-scale data centers that require tens of billions in cabling, favoring decentralized edge infrastructure.

European Sovereignty, Security, and Corporate R&D

  • Infrastructure vs. Data Sovereignty: Beyond cloud and data sovereignty, the Aether model provides infrastructure sovereignty, allowing companies to keep their data on-premise.
  • Protection Against Geopolitical Restrictions: Alain Wilmouth highlighted risks for European companies with partial foreign ownership (e.g., a major European car manufacturer with 32% Chinese ownership and Chinese board members blocked from buying US AI chips).
  • Secure Enterprise AI: The Aether model allows enterprises to run any Large Language Model (LLM) locally via black fiber connections without exposing proprietary R&D or data to cloud or LLM providers.
  • SMB Solutions: Partnerships with companies like Goodwick and Outscale (Dassault System's Numsec Cloud) enable small and mid-sized businesses (SMBs) to maintain data security and avoid tech giants.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Roadmap to 17 AI Factories: Alain Wilmouth stated a roadmap exists to build 17 different AI factories across Europe through partner networks owning land and power, expanding from France through Germany, Czechia, Hungary, Poland, and Romania.
  • Space-bound Servers: Alain Wilmouth revealed that 2CRSi has already validated three servers specifically designed for space operations over the past three years.

Notable Quotes

*"The reality is if you imagine tomorrow that the AI will replace hundreds or millions of job everywhere in the worlds, you have to understand that if the AI is eating all the power available in countries, what will do the rest of the humanity? ... Having today the reflection, when the money is really here... we have to use it not just to make it in our pocket, but really to have a long-term view of how the AI can help to resolve the climate changing."* — Alain Wilmouth

*"This is not innovation just for innovation's sake. It's about seeing a version of a future that we want, applying innovation to it, and making it happen."* — Ethan Pierse

The oldest trick in the book to make your first million

My First Million(930.0K)·51:14·17.0K views·2026-07-23
Sam ParrHost, My First Million Shaan PuriHost, My First Million
TL;DR*"Money talks, but wealth whispers.
Sam Parr and Shaan Puri explore how century-old giants like the Cargill family built massive empires through logistical dominance, and debate how AI and historical economic principles like Jevons' Paradox will reshape modern work and demand. Scale and Scope: Cargill is...

TL;DR: Sam Parr and Shaan Puri explore how century-old giants like the Cargill family built massive empires through logistical dominance, and debate how AI and historical economic principles like Jevons' Paradox will reshape modern work and demand.

The Cargill Empire: Silent Dominance at Planetary Scale

  • Scale and Scope: Cargill is the largest private company in America, 88% owned by the Cargill family, generating $150B a year in annual revenue.
      • The company has produced more billionaires in a single family than any other entity in history.
      • Their operations touch nearly every part of the modern food supply: seeds, fertilizer, grain elevators, shipping barges, meat packing, salt, and corn syrup.
  • Logistics Monopoly: The company began 160 years ago (pre-Civil War) by building physical grain elevators next to railroads, evolving from a grain storage middleman into a planetary-scale logistics titan.
  • Operating Systems: The family enforces an 80/20 rule (reinvesting 80% of profits, paying 20% as dividends) and employs professional CEOs rather than family managers, maintaining a secretive, low-profile corporate culture ("wealth whispers").

Dynasty Building and Family Governance

  • Family Meetings: Shaan Puri highlights the importance of intentional family meetings—treating household culture and financial strategy like formal board meetings rather than keeping children in the dark about money.
  • Legacy Structures: Both hosts discuss how dynastic families (such as the Hearsts and Rockefellers) utilize ironclad legal trusts, clear values, and mutual respect to survive across generations.
  • The Project vs. Empire Dichotomy: Sam Parr notes a fundamental temperament divide between founders who treat businesses as temporary "projects" to build and sell versus those laying brick-by-brick foundations for multi-generational "empires."

Jevons' Paradox and Technological Disruption

  • The Economic Principle: Named after James Jevons, the paradox states that as technology makes resource use more efficient, total consumption and demand increase rather than decrease (e.g., steam engines increasing total coal usage, or the cotton gin dramatically increasing slavery and cotton output).
  • AI and Code: Sam Parr argues that AI will make code creation significantly cheaper, driving a massive, mind-boggling explosion in total software demand rather than a net-negative reduction in jobs.
  • Historical Parallels: The hosts compare current anti-AI sentiment and worker anxiety to historical Luddites smashing mechanical looms during the Industrial Revolution, arguing that human desire to expand and fill space always generates new, unforeseen industries.

Entrepreneurial Blueprints and Historical Case Studies

  • Frederick Tudor ("The Ice King"): Shaan Puri shares the story of Tudor, who shipped blocks of winter ice from Boston to South America before refrigeration existed, creating demand by giving local bartenders free ice to popularize cold drinks.
  • The Trap of Over-Architecting: Shaan Puri reflects on advice from former Twitch boss Emmett Shear, noting that founders frequently waste time building elaborate, over-engineered solutions (like Trello-board bookshelves) instead of "simply solving the problem."

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Narrative Shift on AI: Sam Parr predicts that within the next 6 months, popular smart commentators and public figures will shift their narrative to position AI as a massive net positive, explicitly citing Jevons' Paradox. *(Timeframe: Next 6 months — Sam Parr)*

Notable Quotes

*"Money talks, but wealth whispers. This is wealth whispers shit... who's the founder? What does he look like? How come they're not on CNBC?"* — Sam Parr (on the Cargill family)

>

*"When technology makes a product that already has some demand more efficient, there is turmoil amongst those who are directly impacted."* — Sam Parr

How Supabase Became One Of The Fastest Growing DevTool Companies In The World

Y Combinator(2.3M)·31:21·4.2K views·2026-07-23
Paul CopplestoneCEO, Supabase
TL;DRSupabase is an open-source, Postgres-backed database and developer tools platform founded in 2020 by Paul Copplestone, which grew from an initial open-source side project into a decacorn valued at $10B after raising a $500M round.
The platform has scaled to 10 million developers...

TL;DR

Supabase is an open-source, Postgres-backed database and developer tools platform founded in 2020 by Paul Copplestone, which grew from an initial open-source side project into a decacorn valued at $10B after raising a $500M round. The platform has scaled to 10 million developers across 60+ countries with 360 employees, and currently powers 60% to 90% of all AI agent-generated databases.

Background & Early Ventures

  • Paul Copplestone (Co-founder & CEO): Originally from New Zealand, grew up with an entrepreneur father, and spent only one brief stint in a corporate consultancy before dedicating his career entirely to startups.
  • Previous Ventures:
    • Startup #1: Served as CTO; did not find product-market fit, leading Paul to leave after two years over strategic disagreements with the CEO.
    • Startup #2: Incubated Supabase as a side project inside a different dev tools company for two years before officially launching it out into the market.
  • Why Postgres & Open Source:
    • Paul closely monitored Hacker News three times a day and noticed Postgres trending upward in popularity.
    • Chose open source due to personal philosophy and community-building values; monetization took about a year and a half to begin.

Launch & Developer Experience

  • Early Positioning: Initially tried a real-time Postgres engine, but growth took off only after rebranding and positioning as the "Open Source Firebase Alternative."
  • Initial Target Market: High-growth, venture-backed startups (specifically Y Combinator companies). Approximately over 60% of every YC batch now uses Supabase, though almost no one trusted them during their own batch.
  • Time to Value Metrics:
    • Measured AWS RDS instance spin-up time at around 8.5 minutes.
    • Set a strict internal goal to drop their own setup time under a minute, which eventually reached roughly 5 seconds.
    • Transitioned the developer experience from a visual dashboard/SQL editor to CLIs, MCPs, and Git-backed declarative schemas designed for AI agents.

The AI Era & Agent-Driven Scale

  • Explosive Growth (2024–2026):
    • Growth accelerated dramatically with the rise of AI builders and "vibe coding" tools like Bolt, Lovable, Claude Code, and Codex.
    • Monthly user metrics jumped from 6.5 million to 10 million developers, with conversion rates and user activation accelerating simultaneously despite zero traditional marketing spend.
  • Supabase for Platforms: Built to support platforms like Lovable and Bolt; enables the automated spin-up of millions of databases. Also adapted for enterprise innovation labs to manage and monitor large volumes of databases.

Company Operations & Culture

  • Remote-First Organization: Employs 360 people across more than 60 countries with zero physical offices.
  • AI-Driven Documentation: Because every decision over the past six years has been fully documented in writing across Slack and Notion, their internal AI agents possess institutional memory dating back to day one.
  • Funding & Valuation: Raised a massive series of rounds, culminating in a $500 million funding round at a valuation exceeding $10 billion.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Paul Copplestone: 60% to 90% of all new database launches are now triggered by AI agents.
  • Paul Copplestone: The next major wave of dev tool opportunity will shift from the "build stage" (prompting and generation) to the "operate stage" (self-driving, automated database management, security patches, and uptime maintenance).

T-Mobile Q2 2026 Earnings Call Livestream

T-Mobile(612.0K)·71:25·2026-07-23
Srini GopalanSenior Leadership, T-Mobile
TL;DRT-Mobile delivered an extraordinary Q2 2026, highlighted by a record-high Net Promoter Score (NPS) of 46—the highest ever in wireless across the Big Three carriers—alongside 13% growth in postpaid service revenue and 12% growth in core adjusted EBITDA.
Driven by strong...

TL;DR

T-Mobile delivered an extraordinary Q2 2026, highlighted by a record-high Net Promoter Score (NPS) of 46—the highest ever in wireless across the Big Three carriers—alongside 13% growth in postpaid service revenue and 12% growth in core adjusted EBITDA. Driven by strong underlying business momentum and disciplined execution, management raised full-year adjusted free cash flow guidance to between $18.4 billion and $18.8 billion.

Financial Results & Guidance

  • Postpaid Service Revenue: Grew 13% year-over-year in Q2.
  • Total Service Revenue: Reached 9% growth year-over-year in Q2, with full-year 2026 service revenues expected to be approximately $77 billion (representing 8% growth), and Q3 expected to be approximately $19.3 billion (up 6% year-over-year).
  • Core Adjusted EBITDA: Grew 12% year-over-year in Q2, with full-year expectations between $37.1 billion and $37.5 billion (10% growth at the midpoint), and Q3 expected to be approximately $9.4 billion (up 8% year-over-year).
  • Free Cash Flow & CapEx: Full-year 2026 cash CapEx remains unchanged at approximately $10 billion. Adjusted free cash flow guidance was increased to $18.4 billion to $18.8 billion (an increase of $200 million at the midpoint), driven primarily by lower cash income taxes.
  • Shareholder Returns: Repurchased an incremental $2.5 billion in Q2 and through July 17. Since late 2022, T-Mobile has repurchased 253 million shares, reducing total shares outstanding to 1.07 billion.
  • ARPA & Churn: Postpaid ARPA grew 2% year-over-year (and 3.7% excluding M&A). Postpaid phone churn dropped significantly to 0.85 in Q2. Postpaid net account additions reached 277,000, with full-year net account additions expected between 950,000 and 1,050,000 (anticipating a temporary Q3 churn elevation due to rate plan modernizations, resulting in an expected ~250,000 net additions for Q3).

Network Leadership & AI Innovation

  • Third-Party Accolades: Won "Best Mobile Network" by Ookla for the third straight time; swept every subcategory in OpenSignal's Quality of Experience and Network Performance Awards (making them the most awarded mobile network over the last 5 years); and was named U.S. Test Champion by P3 across all 13 categories tested, including AI Services Champion.
  • AI & Network-Native Apps: Rolled out live translation on beta in Q2 as T-Mobile's first network-native AI application embedded directly into the core. Long-term, the network is positioned to become connective tissue for physical AI with edge inferencing, partnering with industry leaders such as Figure AI.
  • High-Profile Events & Slicing: Utilized network slicing for live broadcast and wireless terminal ticket entry at major events (PGA, US Open Golf). Achieved the fastest network in all 11 U.S. host locations for the World Cup, utilizing AI-driven Dynamic CX and self-organizing networks to autonomously adjust cell sites near crowds.

Broadband & FWA Growth

  • Industry-Leading Broadband: Continued as the fastest-growing ISP in the country, adding upper-400,000s in total broadband subscribers in Q2 with strong ARPUs and winning J.D. Power #1 in customer satisfaction.
  • Fiber Performance: JV fiber rollout is achieving close to 20% penetration over the first 12 months in deployed areas. Fiber and Fixed Wireless Access (FWA) are treated as complementary, because winning a fiber customer frees up cellular fallow capacity elsewhere.
  • Bundling Metrics: Approximately 70% of T-Mobile's broadband customers also bundle mobile with the company.
  • Fallow Capacity Model: FWA traffic consumption is entirely by design under the fallow capacity model, with total network capacity remaining several times larger than current traffic utilization.

Customer Experience & Value Propositions

  • NPS Record: Achieved an all-time high NPS of 46 in Q2—the highest in wireless history across the Big Three.
  • CLV and Port-in Advantage: Customer Lifetime Values (CLVs) grew by healthy double digits year-over-year. Port-in ARPAs continue to exceed port-out ARPAs by approximately 20%, with over 60% of customers on new accounts selecting premium rate plans.
  • Member Month & T-Life: Celebrated 10 years of T-Mobile Tuesdays with "Member Month" in June, ending the quarter with over 30 million monthly active users on T-Life.
  • Financial Services & T-Ads: The T-Mobile Visa credit card launch with Capital One was one of Capital One's most successful co-branded launches and is on track to rank among its leading co-brand programs for new accounts.

Strategic Capital Allocation & Spectrum

  • Spectrum Pipeline: Thoughtfully maintaining a capital envelope considerate of upcoming spectrum opportunities in 2027 and 2028, including C-band 2.0 and 2.7 GHz (unlocked via Chairman Carr's commitment to American 6G leadership).
  • Direct-to-Device (D2D): Starlink D2D usage remains a very small percentage of network usage (0.0002% in regular months, 0.0003% in peak summer months). Conversations regarding the D2D joint venture are progressing rapidly toward a long-form agreement.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Srini Gopalan predicts that over the long term, T-Mobile's network will become the connective tissue for physical AI with inferencing at the edge.
  • Peter Osvaldik affirms that T-Mobile expects to deliver full-year 2026 service revenues of approximately $77 billion (8% growth), core adjusted EBITDA between $37.1 billion and $37.5 billion (10% growth at the midpoint), postpaid account net additions between 950,000 and 1,050,000, and postpaid ARPA growth of 2.5% to 3% (leaning toward the 3% side).
  • Peter Osvaldik notes that Q3 postpaid account net additions are expected to be approximately 250,000 due to anticipated temporary elevated account churn from Q3 rate plan modernizations.
  • Peter Osvaldik states that Q3 service revenues are expected to be approximately $19.3 billion (up 6% year-over-year) and Q3 core adjusted EBITDA is expected to be approximately $9.4 billion (up 8% year-over-year).
  • Srini Gopalan anticipates that the vast majority of future direct-to-device satellite services will be sourced through the newly formed JV.

Notable Quotes

"Consumer traffic consumption is completely by design from the fallow capacity model and capacity is way in excess of current utilization." — *Peter Osvaldik*
"It's not just a question of this year. I mean, the way we've historically thought about price versus volume really centers around CLV and the opportunity to maximize the total amount of value creation..." — *Srini Gopalan*

Our philosophical approach to guidance, as you are seeing play out, is to begin the year with an ambitious and industry-leading guidance range based on our best view at the time, targeting to land at the midpoint of that strong guidance range, and providing updates throughout the year as warranted. Turning to shareholder returns. In addition to our dividend, we're also excited to have repurchased an incremental 2.5 billion in Q2 and through July 17th. If you step back, since beginning our share buyback program in late 2022, we have repurchased 253 million shares and reduced total shares outstanding to 1.07 billion. While we continue to execute share buybacks this year, including with our previously increased authorization, we are also thoughtfully maintaining a capital envelope that is considerate of upcoming spectrum opportunities in both 2027 and 2028, including C-band 2.0 and 2.7 GHz, which represent an opportunity to further cement our network leadership position and provide increased value creation through, for example, additional 5G broadband capacity unlock. To sum it all up, we continue to see strong momentum in the business and cannot be more excited for the future. And so with that, I'll now turn the call back to Cathy to begin the Q&A. Cathy? Jensen Huang says the AI doomers have it wrong

Axios(110.0K)·70:22·53.5K views·2026-07-23
Jensen HuangCEO, NVIDIA Mike AllenJournalist, Axios
TL;DRNvidia CEO Jensen Huang dismissed extreme warnings about artificial intelligence as "complete nonsense," arguing that AI is driving unprecedented productivity and net job creation rather than eliminating human labor.
During an exclusive interview with Axios' Mike Allen in Fort...

TL;DR

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang dismissed extreme warnings about artificial intelligence as "complete nonsense," arguing that AI is driving unprecedented productivity and net job creation rather than eliminating human labor. During an exclusive interview with Axios' Mike Allen in Fort Worth, Texas, Huang also discussed U.S.-China technology competition, the rise of open-source models, and why Wall Street fundamentally misunderstands the current infrastructure buildout.

Geopolitical Competition & Trade with China

  • State of China Sales: Huang stated that Nvidia's sales to China are approximately zero and advised investors to expect zero sales unless the U.S. and Chinese governments permit a return to the market.
  • Chinese AI Models: Praised models like DeepSeek, Kimi, and GLM as excellent tools that advance the global industry. Huang rejected the misconception that Chinese models contain backdoors, noting they operate safely within isolated "sandboxes" and harnesses.
  • Open vs. Closed Models: Argued that while closed models (like Anthropic and OpenAI) are fantastic for everyday use, open-source models are foundational for science, cybersecurity, and national security because transparency allows the global community to inspect and harden them.
  • Government Regulation: Emphasized that regulation should focus on specific dual-use applications (such as autonomous vehicles and medicine) rather than restricting the underlying technology itself.

Market Misconceptions & Wall Street's "CAPEX" Blindspot

  • The Stock Market Drop: Explained that Wall Street panicked over recent Chinese open-source releases (similar to the DeepSeek reaction), incorrectly assuming free AI hurts hardware demand. Huang maintained that "free AI is great for hardware, chips, and data centers" because better models drive higher usage.
  • The New CAPEX Reality: Acknowledged that the new IT industry requires significantly heavier capital expenditure (CAPEX) to build out the intelligence layer (AI factories and data centers), but argued this infrastructure buildout is industrially driven rather than cyclical or seasonal.
  • Lack of Demand Bubble: Stated that the semiconductor industry needs to be 5 to 10 times larger than it is today, with current shortages across memory, storage, optical interconnects, and TSMC foundries.
  • The AI Flywheel: Noted that an inflection point has been reached where AI is both useful and profitable—evidenced by companies gladly paying hundreds of millions for coding agents that dramatically increase productivity.

The Jobs Narrative & Economic Reality

  • Net Job Creation: Refuted predictions that AI will eliminate half of American jobs, presenting evidence of rising headcounts:
    • Radiologists: Increased by roughly 20% due to automated scans allowing doctors to see significantly more patients.
    • Paralegals: Increased by roughly 10% as automated case handling allowed firms to clear massive backlogs.
    • Manufacturing: Increased by some 50% over the last several years to build AI data centers and infrastructure.
  • Closing the Technology Divide: Highlighted AI as the first major technology that closes the technology divide, acting as a personal coach and tutor for anyone regardless of background.
  • Small Corporate Footprint: Shared that despite Nvidia's massive valuation, it employs only 50,000 people and aims to keep headcount "as small as possible" (projecting roughly 75,000 in a decade) compared to tech giants with six-figure headcounts.

Robotics, Agents, and Future Timelines

  • The Robotic ChatGPT Moment: Declared that the "ChatGPT moment" for robotics has already arrived, noting that robots can now reason through complex, multi-step physical tasks when given natural language prompts.
  • The Agentic Era: Predicted that the computing landscape is shifting from 100 million active human users to a future dominated by 100 billion to a trillion software agents running continuously and consuming vast amounts of computer resources.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global Open Source Ecosystem: Huang stated that open-source models will proliferate globally, driven by American, European, Chinese, Japanese, and Indian developers, making openness foundational for security.
  • IPO Milestones: Predicted that OpenAI and Anthropic will grow to go public and become "the most successful IPOs in human history."
  • Bubble Timeline: Asserted that a major infrastructure bubble is very unlikely to occur within the next 5 years, while the 5-to-10-year outlook depends entirely on physical building constraints.
  • Useful Robotics Timeline: Estimated that robots will become practically useful in the everyday lives of ordinary people within 3 to 4 years.
  • Corporate Headcount Projection: Projected that Nvidia's workforce will grow from its current 50,000 to approximately 75,000 employees over the next 10 years.

Notable Quotes

"The fact that this is going to be the end of humanity, it's complete nonsense. The fact that this is going to destroy half of the American jobs is complete nonsense and all of the facts, all of the evidence point exactly to the opposite." — Jensen Huang
"We are laying the foundations, building the infrastructure, the largest industrial infrastructure buildout in human history, so that we can build the foundation of industries in the future for the next generation. And so this is just the very beginning." — Jensen Huang
"America is the greatest country in the world, full stop... because of all those great challenges, because of all those disagreements, we somehow manage to work it through." — Jensen Huang
I'm Mike Allen with Behind the Curtain. Today from Fort Worth, Texas, we're talking to Jensen Huang, CEO of Nvidia, the most valuable company in the world, about what's next for the AI revolution. Jensen Huang, co-founder and CEO from day zero of Nvidia, thanks for sitting down with Axios Behind the Curtain. I'm delighted to do it, Mike. Great to see you. Axios always starts with the news. The Financial Times says Chinese regulators are looking at export controls, tightening their controls on AI models and semiconductors to keep them away from the West. Do you think that will happen, and what would the net effect on the U.S. be? I hope not. The Chinese market and the Chinese industry create some really great AI models and AI technology. I've said before that half of the world's AI researchers are Chinese. Many of them still live in China, and they produce some really groundbreaking research in AI. The United States does too, and many places around the world do. We want to make sure that we can all share ideas as broadly and quickly as possible so that we can advance this incredible technology in a way that's beneficial to society, safely, and so on. I hope that they continue to keep it open. You were just in China a couple of months ago. Do you think they will? What's the likelihood? I don't know. I don't know the source of that news, and there is, of course, all kinds of regulation being discussed. Export control is being discussed on both sides of the ocean, and once one side decides to exercise export controls, everybody decides to think about export controls. I think we all ought to de-escalate and let the technology advance; the companies in the United States are very, very capable, and we're able to move very quickly. I have every confidence that the United States will continue to lead, and so long as we have the support and the encouragement of governments, we should be able to continue to do so. Flip side, should the government ban or restrict Kimi or other Chinese models? I hope not, and I really don't think so. The world needs open and closed models. We should use closed models because they're great to use; they work incredibly well. Anthropic and OpenAI, these are incredibly great services. We should use closed models as much as we can, rent everything we can. However, the industry still needs open models. Science needs open models. Cybersecurity needs open models because it's safe; it's important for cybersecurity, it's important for national security, it's important for economic security. Companies need to be able to control some of the IP and some of the data that they have so that they can advance the AI models in a controlled way. Cybersecurity with the benefit of transparency, the ability for the entire community to inspect and test to make sure that we harden it in the best possible way—it's best for cybersecurity. And so, irrespective of how many industries, open models are foundational to the advance of science and the advancement of computer science today. I really do hope that we keep it open. Simple question on the front page of the Wall Street Journal: should American companies be allowed to use Chinese AI models? Absolutely. There's a misconception that somehow there are backdoors, that it's somehow connected to China in some way. You download the models, you can fine-tune them, you can enhance them, you can guardrail them as you desire. These models don't just operate; they operate within what is called a harness, and that harness sits within what is essentially a sandbox. These sandboxes are secure, these sandboxes keep privacy, security, and access control, and so all of that capability is put into the sandbox. I think these models are capable models, excellent models; they advance the whole industry, and we want AI to diffuse into the market as quickly as possible. Open models allow for that. Can intervention by the government ever be a good thing? Of course, regulation is a good thing in many different industries, and you want to regulate applications. You have to be mindful to regulate technology. The raw technology itself is dual-use, and so we want to make sure that you regulate how it's used. Maybe you want to regulate the service itself. Maybe you'll regulate how it's used in medicine, how it's used in transportation. You should regulate how AI is used in autonomous vehicles. All these different applications of AI should be regulated. However, the technology itself ought to be able to advance as quickly as possible. You've said China is an adversary in AI. What should the rules of engagement be for the U.S. with an AI adversary? Well, the first thing is to recognize that AI has dual-use, and we want to advance it as quickly as possible because the technology, when it's advanced, is more safe, it's more secure, because it works better. However, we want to make sure that the researchers in all of these countries are able to dialogue and cooperate whenever possible, so that we can advance the technology safely. I think an open dialogue is really important, and that should be foundational. Your stock price depends partly on sales to China. Why should we trust what you say about China and the market? Well, we should start with the fact that our China sales are approximately zero today, and I've told all of our investors not to expect any China sales. If both the United States—and the United States has already licensed it—and the Chinese government and the Chinese market would like us to return, it would be a great honor to be able to support the customers and the market with AI and cloud services. That would be great for business. But until then, I would just assume that the sales are zero. That's the policy. Now, Kimi, the very powerful Chinese model that really rattled the stock market. Microsoft is using and testing Kimi. The information says Chinese models may even replace some of Microsoft's use of OpenAI and Anthropic. So this Chinese competition is coming fast and furious. What should U.S. AI companies do? These Chinese models are excellent. Open-source models that are excellent should be used. The market has misunderstood the impact of DeepSeek for the first time. It's misunderstood the impact of Kimi again, this time. I think, first of all, with great AI and open models, it's great for the whole industry. Obviously, when there's more AI, even if it's open, wherever it comes from, there'll be more use. Whenever there's more use, you'll have to sell a lot more Nvidia computers, there'll have to be more data centers, there'll have to be more services. The technology will diffuse into more industries. Starting point is: great models lead to great use, which leads to great growth. That's just the starting point. That happened with DeepSeek. It's going to happen with Kimi. There's also a misunderstanding that open models could be adversarial to the closed models, and that's also got it wrong. The reason for that is because the most likely person to upgrade to a great model like Anthropic and OpenAI is someone who already uses AI. They would like to have it more conveniently; maybe they'd like to have more services that come along with Anthropic and OpenAI. Maybe the models are just so much better, which I expected them to be. I think the thing that we want to do is make sure that we have AI technology diffuse into the industry as quickly as possible, have as many people use it as quickly as possible. Open models, extremely cheap models, easy on-ramps to try AI—that's the best thing we can do. Now, Nvidia has its own open-source model, Nemotron. When we were miking up, you were telling me it's at the frontier, not the frontier. Will it become the dominant U.S. open-source model? We built it so that companies and industries who, for sovereignty reasons, regulatory reasons, privacy reasons, or very specific domain intellectual property reasons, would like to build their own AI. I advise all of my customers to use OpenAI and Anthropic, and use closed models and services as much as they can, because it's easy and it works incredibly well. I use it. I use Perplexity and Anthropic and OpenAI. Use these services as much as you can. You only build your own AI if you must. It turns out a lot of companies, whether it's financial services or cybersecurity companies, companies like ourselves, we must build our own AI. Nemotron is built for companies who need to do that. We don't have to be the frontier; we have to be at the frontier. How quickly could you push out the big boys or compete with the big boys at the frontier? That's not our goal. Our goal is simply, if you need to build your own custom models and custom AI, we could be of service to you to help you do that. With the right harness—these harnesses are, for example, Open Claw or Hermes or Claude, for example—is a harness around the large language models. The large language model is the brain; the harness, if you will, turns it into an agent and into a thinking, working agent that can help you do things. With the right harness, Nvidia's Nemotron is extraordinarily good, and you could adapt it, customize it for the certain skills that you have in mind, and it can be completely world-class. What are the economics for the proprietary labs if open-source models, not just Chinese ones, can produce tokens—the intelligence—cheaper? Fantastic. Really, what the world needs is more people who need AI. The only way that you need AI is because you tried it and you realize you want better ones. These free services and build-it-yourself AIs, it's not for everybody. However, once you try it once and you realize it's so clumsy, it's so much work to build it. So you're saying if I'm OpenAI or Anthropic, I should not be afraid of open models. Not at all. I think that what they need more than anything is just for the whole world to realize they need AI. The way that people want to have better AI is they tried AI today and they just realize it'd be nice for somebody else to help me serve it. It's no different than cloud services, it's no different than search, it's no different than databases in the cloud. We could run our own search, but what a hassle it is. We can, in fact, build our own computers. Why don't we just use it in the cloud? There are a lot of things we could do that are more cost-effective, but it's in fact more convenient to use it when it's in Anthropic and OpenAI. I would also argue that for a lot of people and for a lot of companies, even if the model is free, it will still cost you more to operate it yourself. You might as well have somebody else do it for you. I think that whether it's just more accessible, it's going to be better. However you think about these closed models, I think they're going to be thriving for years to come. What's your vision for the future with open-source and proprietary models competing with each other? The world is going to have closed and open models. We should use closed models as often as we can and we should customize and use open models to build our own personal, our own customized AI if we must. Now, of course, the world is large, and for many countries and companies, they must build their own intelligence. They can't outsource their fundamental intelligence to a third party. You should apply and adopt and rent intelligence from cloud services wherever you can. However, you can't outsource your own intelligence; you should build your own custom AI. The Palantir CEO went viral for his interview warning about giving up your alpha, your intellectual property, to the frontier labs. You're in a unique position. You both use the frontier labs and you supply them. You use them for your own chip design, and they are two of your most important customers. Do you worry about giving away your alpha, your IP, to an AI that may be using it to do their own optimized chip design? Nobody should outsource their alpha; nobody should outsource their intelligence. No country should. We should always import and rent services wherever we can. However, there are areas that are domain-specific, proprietary, maybe sovereign, maybe secret, maybe regulated. I need to do it myself; a company or country needs to do it themselves. However, there's a whole lot of things that we do that are not related to that. Maybe it's really automating marketing or maybe it's really automating our legal department, and in all of those different, many use cases around the company, it's really about enhancing the productivity of the core company itself. It's not specific domain or intellectual property to the company. In those cases, I would outsource as much as we can. What's the scariest or most uninspiring thing that you personally have seen AI do where you said, "Holy shit, I can't believe this is happening"? Well, I expected it to do almost everything that we can do. It wasn't a far reach to imagine that AI can generate videos that are photorealistic and so lifelike. It isn't so beyond imagination that AI can generate text and from that, it can reason. From that, it can essentially write code. The things that AI is doing are fairly consistent with what I expected it to be able to do. It could drive cars, it could move around and manipulate things, pick up things, and essentially, you know, manufacture these computers autonomously. All of those capabilities I've largely imagined. I think the part that the market, the industry, and society needs to realize is that while we're automating all of these different tasks, it's actually increasing the amount of jobs that the world needs, and it's very sensible to me. While people have said that AI is going to eliminate jobs, that it's going to make it so that we don't have to work at all, it's exactly the opposite. The reason for that is—and I'll just first of all give you the evidence—the number of radiologists in the world is increasing substantially, some 20% I understand. The reason for that is because the studying of the radiology scans has become completely automated by AI, but as a result, they could see a lot more patients. And because there are so many people who need care, the number of patients they can now bring to the hospitals has increased dramatically, which results in needing more radiologists. This is the same with paralegals. Apparently, the number of paralegals has increased by some 10%, and the reason for that is while they're using AI, the number of cases they can see has increased and there's just so many cases backlogged that now they need a lot more radiologists and a lot more paralegals to be able to see all these different clients. This is happening all over. The number of manufacturing jobs has increased some 50% in the last several years because these computers have to go into these AI data centers which generate the tokens, the intelligence. Because that is in such great demand, the number of manufacturing jobs has gone up. When you think about AI across the whole industry, jobs are being created everywhere. So one more on what blew your hair back. We know that Anthropic's models can break into a bank, or can break into an intelligence agency. What has surprised you or what's coming that gives you pause? Well, it surprised me that people were surprised. The fact that an agent can generate code is because it can understand software. It can debug software and if it can debug software, it can find vulnerabilities in software. Cybersecurity and finding bugs is not an inconsistent idea. The fact that AI can generate and debug software suggests it could also be used for cybersecurity. The flip side of it is equally true. We need to make sure that open models are in the hands of cybersecurity experts around the world. Open models enhance cybersecurity; open models enhance safety. The reason for that is because we want massively distributed, self-diverse defense. We want to put open models in the hands of cybersecurity experts all over the world so that they can defend themselves, look for vulnerability, and share ideas among communities. If everything just becomes one single model, one single point of attack, one single source of failure, I think the world is much, much more vulnerable. The thinking that somehow we have to close models so that the world could be more safe is exactly the opposite. We have to open the models so that the world could have self-cybersecurity defense. At age 30, 33 years ago, you started Nvidia, which then was a company to bring 3D graphics to video gaming. You've been CEO since day one. You now have the world's most valuable company. It turned out the same technology that could be used in video game cards is used in AI chips. You were worth a trillion dollars three years ago, now Nvidia is worth 5X that, 5 trillion dollars. Could a success story like that ever be done again, or are you a unicorn's unicorn? Anything is possible in the United States. There's a reason why it's called the American Dream. If you're willing to work hard, if you're willing to take chances and go do something unique, the environment, the conditions, the support, the people, the brilliance that's all around us makes it possible. This is the land of dreams; almost anything's possible. AI may make it more possible. Like I was telling my nieces and nephews, great fortunes are going to be created on a laptop. It's all completely true. The number of startups that are being created today is growing extraordinarily fast. In the last 6 months, $300 billion has been invested into venture capital, into startups just here in the United States. $300 billion. All of that is creating new jobs, new companies; it's incredible. Now, Nvidia is facing the toughest headwind since the Great Recession close to 20 years ago. You had a market rout, China rising, the U.S. government looking to be more restrictive. Bringing your engineer's mind to the job of the CEO, how do you navigate all that? The world's changing every day and you just got to stay alert, but I have every confidence that Nvidia and the United States are going to continue to thrive. The confidence comes from not hope, but the fact that you're surrounded by amazing people. The country fundamentally wants to succeed, and it creates the conditions for entrepreneurs and innovators to do great work and we're surrounded by amazing brilliance. We have an incredible local market, we have all the recipes, all the ingredients necessary for long-term success, and so I'm as bullish as ever, as confident as ever. The United States was supposed to be 6 months ahead of China in AI, not much margin for error. Now the tech world has been rocked by Chinese models that rival OpenAI's ChatGPT and Anthropic's Claude. Has China caught up? Whether China has or not, I don't think it matters. Let me tell you why. First of all, the idea that it's a race with an endpoint—obviously, we're going to continue to use AI forever. The United States is going to be here for a long time, China is going to be here for a long time. We have to recognize that China has some of the largest populations of science and math students in the world. If they manufacture anything, that's incredible; they manufacture math students incredibly. They manufacture more AI researchers than the rest of the world combined. The fact that China is going to be extraordinary at this is a foregone conclusion. The idea that we would hold China back is ill-conceived and it's not going to happen. Nor are they going to hold the United States back. This is an incredibly innovative country. We have brilliant minds from all over the world; they come here because of the American Dream, immigrants, American-born, you name it. We have great universities, we have great industry. I have every confidence that the United States will continue to thrive. The most important thing that we have to do with AI is not to scare our industries, not to scare our society into not using AI. AI is not going to destroy all of our jobs. Someone who uses AI is going to take our jobs. We have to make sure that we adopt AI, diffuse AI into the industries as quickly as possible. Anything we do that limits that, anything we do that prevents that from happening—whether it's because we scared people, or we talk all day about the doom of AI—I think all of that is just ill-placed, and it's not helpful to the United States. So long as our industries take advantage of AI, we're going to do incredibly well. On the doom, did some of your fellow moguls over-index on warning people, talking about what could come? I think it's fine to warn people. It's even better to warn people with a solution. It's absolutely inappropriate to make things up. The fact that this is going to be the end of humanity, it's complete nonsense. The fact that this is going to destroy half of the American jobs is complete nonsense. All of the facts, all of the evidence point exactly to the opposite. Intuition and good wisdom would tell you that it's exactly wrong, that AI will increase productivity. When productivity is increased, it creates opportunities. Look, over the history of the arc of time, society has become more and more productive as a result of technology. It has created more jobs, not less. It's created new industries, new opportunities, new markets, new applications. All of that increased the amount of opportunity and growth. Several years into it—it's now 15 years into the era of AI—the evidence is abundantly clear. We're creating a whole bunch of manufacturing jobs because AI requires industrial might. That's created jobs in the energy sector, in the chip sector, in the infrastructure sector—land, power, and shell. It's created a whole new slew of AI companies and of course, the industries that take advantage of AI are also growing. AI is creating all kinds of jobs. One more on China: you took a very realistic point of view about the strength that China will have. So are OpenAI and Anthropic, two of your best customers, in trouble? No, not even a little bit. Both companies are thriving; they're going to continue to grow, they're going to go public. These two companies will be the most successful IPOs in human history. How is it possible that two companies that are only a few years old are worth a trillion dollars? The idea that AI being diffused—abundantly free AI being diffused all over industry, all over society—so that if someone would like to have an off-the-shelf, incredibly built service, instead of making their own AI, instead of hosting their own AI, to have that choice, I think it's going to be fantastic. The more AI is used, the more people use AI, the more people rely on AI, the more opportunity for OpenAI and Anthropic. So there's no scenario where China runs U.S. companies off the road? No. Zero possibility. The American tech industry is too vibrant, too smart, too innovative, moves too fast. Whatever competition there is around the world, bring it on. The United States is ready. After Kimi, the powerful Chinese model, dropped, investors dumped Nvidia shares. Chip stocks are down 18% in the past month. What's Wall Street getting wrong about the future of the AI buildout? Well, free AI should be great for hardware. Free AI should be great for chips. Free AI should be great for data centers. I think the market just got it wrong. They got it wrong with DeepSeek, as you recall, when DeepSeek came out, our stock price was down some 30%. I think this is exactly the same thing. What this industry needs is great AI. The fact that Kimi is so extraordinary, Q1 is so extraordinary, GLM is so extraordinary, Nvidia's NeMoTron are so extraordinary—all of these models that are open are great for the industry, including OpenAI's future models. All of these applications that are doing incredible work, that are useful AI—useful AI has now arrived, and useful AI is also profitable AI. When something is useful, people desire to use it. Closed models are excellent, open models are necessary, and both of us are great for the industry. Some investors think that AI has peaked. What do you know about the technology that you can tell them and the forward direction? It's impossible that AI has peaked because the diffusion of AI into society and into industry has barely begun. What the world is seeing is that this new industry requires CAPEX, unlike the last IT industry, which was very CAPEX light. Software industry was very CAPEX light, which is the reason why software companies had such high gross margins. So, capital expenditures: you have to build things in order to produce the modern software. You need a computer like this to generate the software. When AI is doing the coding, it's this machine that's doing all of the processing of the large language model. It's doing the reasoning and the thinking, it's using all kinds of tools, and to do that, tokens have to be produced. Math has to be produced in this machine. This machine generates the intelligence that we see on our screen when words are coming out or images are coming out, or in the future, proteins and chemicals and robotic systems are articulating and maneuvering. All of that is because this machine is generating the mathematics behind it. The new IT industry, the new software industry, and in the future, every single industry will be CAPEX heavier. Most intelligence industries today are CAPEX light. That's just simply going to change, but what you get as a result is incredible intelligence, incredible productivity, incredible growth. The growth and the profitability and the prosperity that's going to come as a result of intelligence being manufactured is going to more than make up for the CAPEX that we're investing in today. We are laying the foundations, building the infrastructure—the largest industrial infrastructure buildout in human history—so that we can build the foundation of industries in the future for the next generation. This is just the very beginning. Some of the conductors, as you have lived, have always been a boom and bust industry. Are we due for a bust? No, not for a while. The reason for that is this time is different. This time is different because this is not seasonal, not demand driven. This is industrially driven; the fundamental technology of computers is changing. We need a whole new layer of infrastructure in the world. We have energy, we have the internet, we have roads, we have railroads. We now need AI, the intelligence layer, built on top of all of that. This layer needs chips. I believe we probably need our semiconductor industry to be somewhere between 5 to 10 times larger than it is. It is way too small today, which is the reason why memory is in such short supply, storage is in such short supply, optical interconnects are short, packaging is short, and of course, TSMC's foundries are short. Everything is in such short supply because it's not a cyclical nature. It's not demand from consumers; it's fundamental infrastructure-related. The whole industry is short. I think we need to build up the whole industry several more times. Your customers are selling hundreds of billions of dollars in debt to pay for your products. When does the party stop? When does your finance team start to worry about these deals? We're not worried about these deals because of several factors. One, these companies are extraordinary companies and they generate a lot of cash. What they all see simultaneously is that there's a fundamental change in how computing is done. It's a compute platform shift. Whereas computers used to be all content that was pre-recorded—we wrote it in advance, we recorded it in advance, we took a picture in advance—in the future, all of that is going to be foundational information that we generate intelligence on top of. Instead of search, you simply ask a query, you have a question, and it gives you answers. Instead of combing through all these different websites, the AI will generate the answer for you. This future is a whole new way of doing computing that's fundamentally different than the past, and we need a lot more computers. Now, the reason why some of the concerns were: "When is the ROI coming?" We now know that AI is profitable. It's the reason why Anthropic is growing so profitably and so incredibly fast. AI is both useful—that's why we're using it for coding—and profitable. OpenAI wants to manufacture so much more because these coding agents are incredibly profitable; they're doing useful work for very high-paying jobs. Many companies like ourselves are happy to pay hundreds of millions of dollars a year so that we can go use these AI services to help us augment our coding capability. We become more productive, we innovate more, we create more growth, and we become more profitable, which pays for more AI. The flywheel has now started. The industry has now arrived at a very critical juncture, an inflection point, where we now have profitable AI because we have useful AI, and because of that, we want to build a lot more AI. Here's the crux. You've defined a token as the fundamental unit of artificial intelligence. What convinces you that a token is going to keep getting more profitable? The token is what is called an embedding. It embeds knowledge; it embeds intelligence. That number, that token that's being generated by this machine, is not a static number—it's not like PI. That number encodes smarter and smarter intelligence over time. Surprisingly, those trillions and trillions of numbers are getting smarter and smarter over time. When they get smarter over time, they become more useful, more valuable. When they become more valuable, people pay more for it. I know that for most people, the concept of numbers is hard to be valuable, but these tokens embed intelligence, and the intelligence is getting smarter and smarter all the time. Every industrial buildout in history has seen a bubble over-investment. What is this generation's bubble danger? The bubble will come someday, it's just not today. This is in the very beginning of the buildout. It is very unlikely in the next 5 years, and we'll see about the 5 to 10 years; it just depends on how fast we can build. The challenge, of course, is that the rate at which we can build the infrastructure is limited by physical things. Today, the industry could build faster, it could run faster, but we don't have enough chips, we don't have enough memory, we don't have enough land, power, or construction workers to build the data centers. We are constrained in every single direction and in every single way. That constraint is good; that constraint is what holds the system back so that it gives us plenty of time to build out this infrastructure. On the one hand, there's a great desire to invest the CAPEX; on the other hand, the ability for that CAPEX to land and turn into productive supercomputers like this and into AI factories is delayed because of all these different constraints. I think it's going to push out the time when supply exceeds demand for some time. Would you use Kimi, the Chinese open-source model? Of course, it's smart. You still have to make sure that you fine-tune it, you safeguard it, you guardrail it, you put it inside the appropriate sandboxes, and put security around it. Just like all software, we download software from open source all the time. One of the most important pieces of software in history is the operating system. It controls the computer; it literally controls humanity today. It's the control fabric around the whole world's digital systems. It is open source, based on Linux. It has the opportunity for millions and millions of people to study it, to interrogate it, to test it, to make sure that it's hardened. As a result, we can trust it. Open source is a fantastic way for us to harden and trust the infrastructure. We really need to have open-source models; it's essential for society, essential for industry, and it's also the safest and the most secure way to go forward. It's fantastic that Kimi is great, and it's fantastic that there are American, European, Chinese, Japanese, and Indian open-source models. I'm working with many of them; that openness is fantastic and great for safety, security, and national security. You're arguably the most beloved AI CEO; people line up for your autograph when you're in Asia, paparazzi follow your car, people want you to sign their clothing. Why is the view of AI so different in Asia than here? Maybe because the doomers spend too much time theorizing about these science fiction outcomes—maybe it makes them sound smart. I think it makes them sound smart. I think leaders ought to be thoughtful about doing this. If you want to warn the world about the incredible capabilities of this technology, that has been achieved. We should dedicate our time to how we make the technology safe. That's our responsibility as the technology leaders. Telling everybody that the AI technology has to be safe is just reminding yourself you have to make it safe. We have to make sure that people understand the capabilities of this technology and advance it in a way that's secure, safe, and working functionally. The warning is well-heeded to make sure that regulators and policy makers are able to understand the nature of this technology. On the other hand, we want to encourage the American people to use this technology. We led this industrial revolution not because we invented all the technology, but because we applied it with such enthusiasm that this industrial revolution led to the United States. We need to make sure that we apply this technology with great enthusiasm in healthcare—a doctor at Johns Hopkins just wrote that AI technology is helping advance discovery of cancer a lot more quickly. We know what it's done for radiology, we know what it's doing for software engineering. Software engineering is the fundamental engine of intelligence today, the fundamental engine of automation and productivity today. We know what it's able to do in helping us advance climate science. Because of AI, we're able to upgrade our national power grid. Because of AI, we're able to invest in sustainable energy without government subsidies. All of this is because of this particular industry. We ought to be much more enthusiastic about it and help the United States realize that the only way we get left behind is if we don't apply the technology. Forget who contributes and invents the technology; the fact that we don't apply it, that's how we're going to get left behind. Let's make sure we remind ourselves of how we became the United States in this modern industrial revolution and apply exactly the same enthusiasm, the same American spirit, so that we can lead the next one. You have to advance the technology safely, make it a contribution to society, always think about other people's interests, and make sure that we develop this technology in a way that helps society and industry broadly. One of the things that I like to remind people is that of all of the technologies we've ever invented, this one closes the technology divide more than any other. You can prove it. This is the first amazing technology in history where anybody can use it. That's the reason why so many people have used ChatGPT. In every industrial revolution, some tasks and some jobs become automated. Even if more net jobs are created, what are the jobs and tasks that will go away like in any other industrial revolution? That's right, exactly. That's exactly right. Look, today, most of the people in IT and in technology sit in front of a keyboard typing. Powering Agentic AI with AI-Ready Data Platforms That Turn Data Into Intelligence

NVIDIA(2.2M)·36:04·2.0K views·2026-07-23
Jason HardyVP of Storage Technology, NVIDIA
TL;DRJason Hardy (VP of Storage Technology at NVIDIA) explains how the shift toward agentic AI requires enterprise storage to evolve from a static system of record into a dynamic system of context and a new data plane.
This transformation relies on AI-ready data platforms,...

TL;DR

Jason Hardy (VP of Storage Technology at NVIDIA) explains how the shift toward agentic AI requires enterprise storage to evolve from a static system of record into a dynamic system of context and a new data plane. This transformation relies on AI-ready data platforms, specialized memory tiers for KV cache, and co-designed NVIDIA technologies like Vera and BlueField-4 STX working alongside storage partners.

The Three Pillars of Enterprise Data & Agentic Demand

  • Structured Data: Comprising small volumes from ERP systems, SQL databases, and business systems where core operational decisions are made.
  • Unstructured Data: Representing the lion's share of enterprise volume—including home directories, SharePoint, OneDrive, Google Docs, NAS shares, PDFs, video, and audio—noted as the hardest to process due to diverse data types.
  • Agent Context (The New Category): Encompassing KV cache, agent memory, scratchpads, to-do lists, and embeddings generated as agents consume information and execute tasks; some agents operate for hours while others persist for years.
  • New Operational Paradigm: Storage must transition from stateless, short-lived interactions to handling long-running, 24/7 sessions where tens to thousands of agents iterate over blended structured and unstructured datasets at speeds vastly exceeding human reading rates.

Data Preparation & The AI Data Platform

  • Overcoming Enterprise Hallucinations: High-profile failures (Air Canada, UnitedHealth, Google, Deloitte) are fundamentally data preparation issues rather than purely AI model flaws.
  • Complex Ingestion Pipeline:
    • Multimodal Ingestion: Processing text, tables, and imagery within documents like PDFs concurrently.
    • Cleaning & Enrichment: Removing duplication, normalizing data, stripping out PII and sensitive data, and attaching descriptive metadata.
    • Chunking & Vectorization: Breaking data into semantic units, embedding them into vector databases, indexing, and governing them.
  • In-Place AI Data Platforms: NVIDIA is collaborating with partners like Dell, NetApp, IBM, and HPE to embed AI-ready capabilities directly into enterprise storage systems at the time of creation.
  • NVIDIA Blueprints: Utilizing the RAG blueprint to streamline vectorization and embedding, and the VSS (Video Search and Summarization) blueprint to help agents understand video context directly within the storage system.

Memory Hierarchies and KV Cache Innovation

  • Memory Tiering Architecture:
    • G1 (HBM): Fastest memory directly inside the GPU, holding active KV cache and model weights.
    • G2 (System Memory): Tightly coupled memory collaborating with HBM for model staging and spillover.
    • G3 (Scratch): NVMe disk inside each server for operating systems.
    • G3.5 (CMX): A new dedicated tier introduced by NVIDIA specifically for KV cache management, sitting between high-performance storage and GPU memory.
    • G4 (High-Performance Storage): Traditional high-performance storage, object storage, and enterprise storage where models live and checkpointing occurs.
  • KV Cache Efficiency: CMX stores and serves prefill and decode stage token computations so GPUs spend zero time recomputing existing cached data, maximizing token-per-second output across the AI factory.

Hardware Foundations & Co-Design

  • Vera CPU Integration: Designed to take bits, bytes, and packets off the wire and feed them to the GPU with 3x memory bandwidth and best-in-class single-threaded performance, directly benefiting storage workloads.
  • NVIDIA Vera BlueField-4 STX Platform: An opinionated storage design acting as the foundation for the AI factory, combining NVIDIA software, Vera/BlueField-4 STX hardware, and storage partner ecosystems.
  • Horizontal & Vertical Co-Design: Integrating network (Spectrum-X), compute (Vera), GPUs (Rubin/RTX Pro), and storage platforms to scale agentic AI safely while maintaining enterprise trust, security, compliance, and hybrid on-prem/cloud governance.

Notable Quotes

"Storage needs to move from being what we've known as a system of record to now a system of context." — Jason Hardy
"If you don't address the data accurately, if you don't bring the right data to the context, things can go a little sideways... This isn't an AI issue. This is a data issue." — Jason Hardy
"Every enterprise, every AI factory needs an AI data platform... It's about taking that data, preparing it at the edge at the time of creation in the storage system, and taking that refined good and bringing it to the AI factory so that I can manufacture intelligence." — Jason Hardy
Thank you, Dennis. Good morning, everyone. Welcome to day three of GTC Taipei 2026. I like that you're all the early birds. You got here first, you get all the good content today. So I have a bit of a tradition every time I do this. I have to take a selfie with you all. So pose, smile, jump, do whatever you want, but on the count of three, here we go. One, two, three. All right, thank you. Well, good morning, like Dennis said. My name is Jason Hardy, I'm the vice president for storage technology here at NVIDIA. It's funny being the storage guy in a company that doesn't sell storage. So it's been very exciting though, and we're finding that data continues to play a monumental role in the success of AI, especially as we start to see agentic capabilities really start to grab a hold and take place. There's no denying that from an investment perspective, we're seeing a continuation of investment into AI technologies and the necessary infrastructure to be able to get to these outcomes. This will continue to happen for many years to come and we're really excited about this. Now, AI, though, inferencing on the agentic side, or just even simple engagement just with a chatbot, it relies on a grounding in the data that sits behind it. The data is what describes the personality of an enterprise. It describes what we do and how we operate personally, but also how companies, businesses, enterprises, how they exist, what they do, how they operate. All of that is grounded in data. The problem that we're starting to see is that in order for the success of AI to really grab a hold, it's becoming increasingly dependent on data. We see the facts and statistics around the AI projects that are abandoned or that never see past proof of concept, but ultimately at the end of the day, AI is as good as the data that's fed to it, that it's used against it, that it's trained against. And especially as we look at this agentic frontier that we're starting to embrace and we're really starting to see some amazing capabilities come from, the requirements from a data perspective—especially on the enterprise storage systems, the things that have maintained that data for 50 plus years—the dependency on that is changing those requirements. It's changing how those systems are built, it's changing how those systems operate, it's changing the requirements and the services that even need to come from those platforms and systems. You see, when we look at an enterprise, it's really made up of three different kinds of data. There's structured data, while small in volume, very critical to how an enterprise operates. This is the ERP systems, the SQL systems, the databases, the structured data that business decisions are made off of. Then there's the unstructured data side. Your home directory, SharePoint, some people love it, some people hate it, OneDrive or Google Docs or NAS shares or PDFs or video or audio or files. That is unstructured data, and that is by far the lion's share, the largest volume of data in an enterprise. This also is the hardest to process because it is made up of so many different personalities and so many different data types and ways to engage with it that it is the hardest, but the largest volume of data that describes an enterprise. And now we have this third category, this third classification of data. This is new. This is what's come from the agentic push and the embracing of this technology, and this is context, agent context. Some people know context is KV cache. It's actually more than that. KV cache is a piece of it, but it's also the memory, the data that gets generated as agents operate and as they consume information that we feed them and how they approach the task at hand and everything. That's the data that the agent is responsible for consuming, whether it be what we feed it and it activates off of, or what it generates itself, and even at times shares from agent to agent. This has to persist over long term; some agents live for hours, others live for years. So this data becomes as critical as the rest of the enterprise ecosystem, as critical as the structured data that we continue to maintain with the tools and applications, or even the unstructured data that we generate on a daily basis as we just go about our day, our job. Across these three landscapes, this data here is the foundation of what enterprises are built on. But you see, from an agent demand perspective, there is a new paradigm that's happening, especially for storage. Traditionally we had stateless environments, we had the structured data, we had a user doing a thing and it generated a workflow. And then we also had kind of two states for data to manage: data at rest, what's living there, it could be an archive, or data in motion, that active data, that data set that we operate against where we're modifying or manipulating. Now as we shift into the agentic frontier though, it changes this whole paradigm. Now we aren't just stateless where it's short-lived, now we have long-running sessions. Who here has used agents in one way shape or form? You guys really need, you're at an NVIDIA conference, come on, you should know, you should be using this stuff. These tools are amazing, and in fact, even as I use my own agents, I have the liberty to give it a task, to go to bed, to wake up, and it's worked all night, that task is done, we review and then we iterate from there. So it's no longer just "I end my day and then data's done"—it continues. I'm also blending unstructured and structured data together for these agents to operate. So it's as much SQL as it is a PDF. It's not just me engaging with my data; it's the one, the tens, the hundreds, even the thousands of agents that will continue to iterate over data, continue to consume it. And it will consume it not at the rate at which a human interfaces with data, but at the rate at which agents can start to interface with both the data and each other. And trust me, it's a lot faster than you reading a document. So we have scale, we have new data types, we have data that's active 24/7, and this growing context of needing to keep both, yes, KV cache, but also all of the memory and the scratch and the to-do and the embeddings and everything that goes on to build context around how agents operate. Everything that agentic workflows are starting to push into that we're starting to embrace on a day-to-day basis is changing the landscape of what storage actually means. And so storage needs to evolve. It needs to move from being what we've always known as a system of record to now a system of context. Now, what makes this hard for the enterprise? Obviously things change very quickly. So we need to be able to do things at scale. We need to be able to scale cache, we need to be able to scale those embeddings, that context; everything about how agents operate brings more scale. Performance and density. We also need to introduce new technologies into the enterprise, things like RAG, or retrieval augmented generation, or embeddings and the ability to take all of that data and embed it into vector databases. How do we chunk that up, how do we operate with that? We need to scale those workloads so that our agents are fed at the speed at which they operate. And we cannot forget about the security, the governance and even how enterprises operate today from a hybrid perspective. You see, enterprises aren't just in the data center, and they're not just in the cloud. They're on both. So the question is how do we best enforce and represent the data where it needs to be, on-prem, cloud, wherever, but also ensure that we do this securely and we do this in a controlled manner, do this in a way that we can understand and audit it if ever necessary. We still have many enterprise requirements that need to be maintained, but also need to evolve forward to address the agentic demand. This is why we believe that storage is moving from that system of record to really the new data plane. It's the new data plane for agentic AI. You see, much like Jensen has talked about the AI layer cake, the five layers of AI, storage has much the same. But there's three of them, so maybe it's a cupcake, the cupcake of storage. But what it means is it's based on high-performance requirements, still having enterprise data, but then how do we bring agentic context into that. When we look at it from a storage perspective, just at the raw capability to introduce and support the high-performance requirements, those continue to scale upward in both how we train and inference, but also how do we bring more context in volume, how do we support inferencing at that scale from a performance and demand. This is why we're extremely excited that Vera is here. Vera was designed to take stuff off the wire, information, bits and bytes, packets, process it through and then give it to the GPU as quickly as possible. So the 3x memory bandwidth plays a big role in being able to do that. The single core, single threaded performance improvements, best in class, is great for the GPU. Well you know what else it's great for? Storage. Vera, through the capabilities that we built to be able to drive the GPU, benefits storage the same way. So as we look at now introducing Vera to the market, the NVIDIA Vera BlueField-4 STX platform, our opinionated design of what storage looks like, benefits from all of those capabilities. It's the foundation for how we'll continue to build the AI factory and in working with our partners.
