It's how we bring Vera, it's how we bring our software, it's how we bring all the greatness of the NVIDIA ecosystem plus our storage partners' capabilities, bring those together and release that into the market to be able to address this demand from the agentic workload. Second though, it's not just an infrastructure thing. It's also about how we prepare the data, how we govern the data, how we bring lineage and indexing and everything necessary for enterprise data to be agentic ready. This second layer of the storage cupcake is about addressing the challenges to make sure that we don't become another headline. You see, if you don't address the data accurately, if you don't bring the right data to the context, things can go a little sideways. Air Canada got in trouble with the Canadian government because they had a chatbot hallucinate new refund policies; it didn't exist, the courts didn't care. UnitedHealth got sued for faulty AI and in that had to pay out a significant amount of money. Google had to pay a tremendous amount of money because of wrong answers, because of wrong demos. The same with Deloitte and same with on and on and on and on. This isn't an AI issue; this is a data issue. And this is why data needs to be especially prepared to be able to address the AI demand. Now, it's complicated to do this. There's no denying that. First you have to ingest your data, and this could be whatever type of data, it could be PDFs, it could be tables, it could be structured data, it could be audio, it could be video, it can be very multimodal. The last time you opened up a PDF, when was it just text? Never. So you need to understand not just that there's text in a document, but you have to be able to understand and process through the text and the tables and all of the imagery and how it relates to each other. So you go through this ingestion process. Then you have to clean your data so that it's ready. You have to remove duplicity, you have to remove or normalize the data. Sometimes you have to strip the PII, the sensitive data out of it. And then you want to enrich it, you want to bring metadata to it, that way you have data that describes the data. Then chunk it, break it up, make sure it fits into these semantic units, sized appropriately for the model that you're running, and then you embed it into the vector database and then you index it, and then you govern it on top of it. It's not straightforward. We realize that for data to be properly processed for AI readiness, it takes work, and it's complicated. You see, agents need accurate data and unfortunately data has all of this complexity with it. It has pipeline complexity. It has structured complexity—how do we associate, again, in a PDF, this graph and this text and all of this stuff, how does it correlate with each other? And then data changes over time, it's not static, just like our businesses change over time, just like our enterprises do. And then data has gravity, data has mass. Turns out that the best bandwidth in the world is actually FedEx. Go Google it, it's kind of a meme. But really when we talk about data, data is a complex ecosystem that continuously changes, just like we do, just like our enterprises do. A radiological diagnosis, if something goes wrong, that can impact a patient's health. Or how do we use this technology now to reduce the errors from a radiology perspective? You see, this agentic outcome when we feed it accurate data can give us a significant upside. As the agentic harness that Jensen talked about during his keynote, as it starts to process data and as agents create that context and go through this loop and then act on the end of it and we feed more data in and tools and all the governance and security and then ultimately create that memory that it lives from, all of that requires a new paradigm, a new approach for the enterprise data ecosystem to be able to address this demand. So all of this now, it's not just an enterprise storage problem, but is an AI data platform capability, and this is what enterprise storage is becoming, the AI data platform, and we at NVIDIA have been working with our partners in building exactly that. The AI data platform, it's about bringing that readiness at time of creation, at the source of it from a CPU perspective starting, but including now the ability for GPUs to be added in to the enterprise storage ecosystem, so that now we can have all of that amazing functionality for vectorization, all the embeddings, all the even inferencing at that data directly inside the storage platform. So that idea of understanding data at that time of creation so that my agent can be more effective, it's not just an idea, it's being put into execution. The last layer of the cake, the cupcake, the frosting, context. Addressing context is not easy. In fact, addressing context required us to think about the structure of memory inside of a data center, inside of an AI factory, inside of a GPU. So we created this kind of hierarchy of memory here, that allows us to describe what type of memory lives where. G1 is HBM. It's the fastest memory, it's directly in the GPU, it's where the active KV live, it's where the weights get loaded. Everything about the model and inferencing happens in G1. But G1 is finite. So G2 is system memory. That tightly-coupled memory where both the HBM G1 and the system G2 can collaborate or can work together in as fast of as fast as possible. This is where KV Cache spillover happens, this is where staging happens for models, for inferencing and all sorts of different things. G3 is scratch, that NVMe disk in each server, it's where the OS lives, simple things like that. And then G4 is high-performance storage, where models live, where checkpointing happens, where inferencing takes place. G4 even extends into enterprise storage. Now, when we were looking at context, what we found is that G4 wasn't quite fast enough. And G3, there wasn't quite enough of it. So we created G3.5, and this is where KV Cache lives. Now, KV Cache is really important because when we are inferencing, we have to calculate a lot of information on the prefill and the decode stages. This is how inferencing happens. And the thing about KV Cache is, when you cache that information, you don't have to recalculate it, which is great. That means the GPUs can spend more time working on new data, creating new tokens, instead of having to recalculate, which is awesome. That means that we get the best out of the factory. So, in order to address KV Cache, we needed something that was fast enough to support the model or the GPU without having to recalculate, but also was cost-effective and didn't create too much burden to be able to do that inside the factory. So from our perspective, KV Cache, this is where we've released CMX, our KV Cache storage capability. This new tier of storage ensures that we can offer tokens, offer cache to the GPU as quickly as possible, but without the extra burden, without being too inefficient, all of the great stuff that's necessary to make this effective. That addresses KV Cache though. Like I said earlier, there's another type of context. This memory context, how an agent operates and the data it generates when consuming data and when working with other agents, and everything about it, that's not just tokens. That cache—Jensen talked about it earlier during the keynote—was, it's the ontology. It's how agent's information, and how we are described as users of this, that how that permeates through the system. That context, we're still doing a lot of work on. But we do know that we can make the factory as efficiently as possible by bringing in this KV Cache concept. So this new tier of storage, again, in working with our partners, allows for better scale of the AI factory from a token throughput perspective. More tokens, more intelligence. It also scales to meet the demand of as the factory grows. Start small, go big, support again that workload as we continue to scale the factory size. It's also designed to complement both GPU memory and network storage by sitting in between. It doesn't replace HPS or G4, it augments it. It brings the right type of storage for the right platform, or the right requirement. So KV Cache is really designed to address that acceleration and that token per second output from the AI factory. But again, this is designed not by us but with our partners. So we'll continue to go through this with them, we'll continue to address this, and you'll start to see this permeate the AI factory. Because there is value in being able to manage KV Cache more appropriately, to reuse it as much as possible so that the GPU doesn't spend time recomputing when it can create new. So when we look at the challenges of the data center now, when we look at the challenges of the data ecosystem now, it's not about just bringing storage in. It's not about just bringing a GPU in. It's about extreme co-design across both the stack horizontally—I should say, vertically and horizontally. It's how we bring network, and how we bring GPU, and how we bring compute, especially with Vera now, it's how we bring Spectrum-X in, all of this working together to accelerate and provide as much value as possible. Just the parallel file system, high performance storage requirement. This here is NVIDIA BlueField-4 STX, NVIDIA Vera BlueField-4 STX. "Not just small men" - how healthcare is failing women, and what can be done

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·30:03·253 views·2026-07-23
Lucy PerezPartner, McKinsey Health Institute
TL;DRWomen spend 25% more of their lives in poor health than men—disproportionately during prime working years (ages 20 to 60)—driving a $1 trillion annual global economic loss.
A report by the World Economic Forum and the McKinsey Health Institute outlines three cost-effective,...

TL;DR

Women spend 25% more of their lives in poor health than men—disproportionately during prime working years (ages 20 to 60)—driving a $1 trillion annual global economic loss. A report by the World Economic Forum and the McKinsey Health Institute outlines three cost-effective, high-return care delivery interventions in pregnancy, screening, and mental health that can close this gap and deliver a 3x to 6x return on investment.

The Women's Health Gap: Scale and Economic Impact

  • Staggering Health Disparity: Women spend 25% more time in poor health than men, a gap historically misattributed to end-of-life longevity but actually most pronounced during prime working years between the ages of 20 and 60.
  • Massive Economic Loss: This health gap takes women out of the workforce, resulting in a lost trillion dollars of economic opportunity each year.
  • Proportionate Burden of Non-Gender-Specific Conditions: While pregnancy is a major physiological stress, the majority of the health gap ties to conditions impacting both sexes disproportionately or differently, such as heart disease.
  • Heart Disease Fatality Rate: Women are 50% more likely to die in the year following a heart attack than men, largely due to a medical history of treating female biology as "atypical."

Three High-Impact, Cost-Effective Care Delivery Interventions

  • 1. Breast Arterial Calcification via Mammograms:
    • Over 200 million women get mammograms annually. These images can simultaneously detect breast arterial calcification (calcium buildup in arteries), indicating future cardiovascular event risk.
    • Standardizing and annotating existing mammograms costs practically nothing additional, yet enables early cardiology referrals to avert major downstream heart events.
  • 2. Pregnancy Complications as Cardiac Early Warnings:
    • Hypertensive disorders like preeclampsia and gestational diabetes make women twice as likely to develop serious heart disease later in life.
    • Currently, health systems monitor these conditions during pregnancy but abandon proactive follow-up postpartum, allowing heart disease to surface aggressively decades later.
    • Financial ROI: Improving follow-up and long-term care costs $400 to $450 per woman, far lower than the $100,000+ cost of treating a major cardiovascular event. In the US alone, this averts $6 million to $25 million in costs, representing a 3x to 5x return on investment.
  • 3. Perinatal Depression Screening and Integration:
    • Postpartum depression impacts roughly 20% of women, but focusing solely on the postpartum period is too narrow. Evidence shows manifestation often begins earlier in pregnancy, requiring a holistic "perinatal depression" approach across the entire pregnancy and up to one year post-delivery.
    • Leveraging clinically validated screening tools integrated directly into routine prenatal care de-stigmatizes the condition and protects the health of both mother and baby.

Systemic Barriers and the Role of AI in Healthcare

  • Siloed Health Systems: Connecting diagnostic data across separate medical departments and budgets is challenging, which explains why these common-sense care integrations have not happened automatically.
  • The Data Foundation for AI: While artificial intelligence garners massive enthusiasm, AI models are only as good as their underlying data. Historically, datasets (even during COVID-19) frequently failed to code for sex as a biological variable.
  • Clinical Decision Support: True technological advancement requires clinical software tools to recognize female-specific presentation patterns (such as different heart attack symptoms) immediately upon identifying a patient as female.

Personal Insights and Medical Education Blind Spots

  • Diagnostic Biases: Medical schools historically failed to teach sex-based differences, falsely training clinicians that women are merely "smaller men" requiring only adjusted medication dosages.
  • Research and Diagnostics Discrepancy: McKinsey partner research shows that conditions unique to women are diagnosed much less frequently than male-specific conditions—a disparity widened when male providers perform the diagnosis.
  • Endometriosis Neglect: Endometriosis impacts 1 in 10 women (the exact same prevalence as diabetes), yet while diabetes has over 500 drugs in clinical development, endometriosis has zero approved drugs and only about 20 drugs in clinical development.

Notable Quotes

"Women spend 25% more time in poor health than men do. And many people think that that makes sense, that because women live longer than men, that they would spend more time in poor health because it's concentrated at the end of life. But actually, that big health gap happens during women's prime working years." — Robin Pomeroy
"Investing in preventative care such as integrated screenings and standardized reporting could create health care system savings delivering a three to six times return." — Lucy Perez
"For too long, when we thought about it is we have to adjust the dose because women on average are physically smaller than men and that takes care of it. I think fortunately, what we're seeing now is that people are understanding we're talking science. We're talking human biology." — Lucy Perez

And so here you have a couple of instances where you're getting that alert, right, like that flashing red light, we should be paying attention a little bit more closely because we can help that woman live healthier longer. I'll cite these figures. I mean, you probably know them off the top of your head, but I'll just read from the report here. Improving follow up and long term care costs about $400 to $450 per woman, far lower than the cost of treating a major cardiovascular event, which you're talking about upwards of $100,000. In the US alone, this could avert between six and $25 million in avoided costs, a roughly a three to five times return on investment, which was my initial question. Exactly. How do you come to that figure? Well, that's how you do. Let's look at the third one, then, which is perinatal depression. Just tell us what that is and what you found there. So perinatal would mean the whole period before, during, after, that? Exactly. From the moment basically the woman is pregnant, all the way, typically to the year after delivery is what we're looking at. Well, the reality is that health systems are very complex, as I think we all know and experience it every day. Those things are not easy to do. Typically in health systems they're part of different departments, different budgets. And so what we've tried to do, working with the consortium members, is actually integrating in one place what is the suite of changes that need to happen, and by the way, recognize that health systems really vary in terms of the resources they have. Some are very tech-enabled, right? So in the work that we did, we really tried to solve for different levels of maturity, resources, sophistication that health systems have, so that we can bring not only a perspective on, "Look at all the value at stake," but look at the changes that need to happen in terms of the capabilities you have, the processes that you need to change, and the technology that you want to put in place to facilitate that more seamless journey. Because even during COVID, there were data sets that were not coded for sex. If you don't do that coding, you don't know if there's a sex-based difference. If we're not tracking that data from a sex-based perspective, we cannot have the doctor who's using clinical decision support software, is looking at how that woman manifests when she shows up to the emergency room, and he may think she has indigestion, because the symptoms are not matching the male physiology, right? And so I get very excited about, especially in places like these where we have the evidence, how is that evidence translating into better clinical decision support tools? That, for example, one of those first variables should be, "Oh, I'm talking to a female patient." What should I be looking for? It may be different than what I would be looking for if I'm looking at a male. So, Lucy, tell me kind of how your journey towards this happened? Did you have an aha moment when it was like, "Oh my goodness, this is just not being taken into account by clinicians, by researchers, the fact that women are different from men in a lot of these things?" You know, was there a moment? Is there an incident that happened? Did you read a paper? Did you have a personal experience you thought, "Hold on a minute, you know, that woman found out she didn't have indigestion, she had heart disease" or whatever it was? Part of it at first is a collection of small moments that happen, but then indeed, I will speak to two things that happened. One is, I've always been very passionate about health equity. And one of the things that always struck me when we were looking at health equity is that we didn't really talk about sex and gender as a variable in health equity. And so that made me very curious to start looking at the data. And so many years ago, So what were you talking about then when it comes to equity? There's a much more focus on racial disparities or geographic disparities, which are extremely important, right? But back then, what struck me was, why are we not also talking about sex and gender, right? And so that took me down a path with some colleagues in having conversations about this. I'm a scientist by training, you know, I have a PhD in organic chemistry, so asking questions is something that comes very, very naturally to take a look at some of the data around conditions that are unique to women and unique to men, and understand, do we see any differences in how they're diagnosed, or does the sex of the doctor doing the diagnosis change that answer. And this paper, what we showed was that actually conditions that are unique to women are diagnosed much less frequently than those that are unique to men. And then when you double click on what was the sex of the provider doing that diagnosis, you see that men tend to diagnose male-specific conditions in the same rate that women diagnose male-specific conditions. But when you're looking at women-specific conditions, male doctors diagnose them a lot less. And so that was probably one of for me, like, seeing that data, it really struck me. Then the next thing was having a friend who suffered from endometriosis, really wanting to learn more about this condition because until I got to, in a way, live it side by side with her, frankly, I didn't know much about it. And now, I'm embarrassed that I didn't know more about it earlier. Because endometriosis impacts one in ten women. It's actually the same numbers as diabetes. One in ten women suffer from diabetes, but yet you look at diabetes and we have so many options to treat, right? We don't, you know, there's still plenty of room for more innovation, but you know, there's multiple products in the marketplace, more than 500 drugs in clinical development. And yet you look at endometriosis and you say, okay, same numbers, one in ten women, and yet no approved drugs and only about 20 clinical drugs in development. It's a very stark difference. And so that was the beginning of wanting to know more. And I think then the big insight for me was it is about so much more than the conditions that are unique to women. And that is when you realize, for example, heart disease was probably the most shocking to me, that we just don't talk enough about those sex-based differences in heart disease. And again, that's the number one killer of women. And when you start going through the data, you just find over and over again, that sex-based differences are much more prevalent than not. But yet, for so long, we've operated in a world that assumes there are no sex-based differences. So, if we want better health outcomes for everyone, because by the way, sometimes it is to men's, you know, disadvantage on the sex-based differences. So, if we want better health for everyone, we really need to put that study of sex-based differences front and center. Endometriosis, we did an episode of Radio Davos on that, moving back two or three years now. If you're listening to this and you have never heard of endometriosis, a lot of people haven't, but as you say, it's very, very common and it's chronically underdiagnosed. Most women almost never get it diagnosed first, second, third time they visit the doctor. I'd invite people to go back and listen to that episode. I wonder, when you have conversations about this issue now, does it ever kind of butt up against gender politics and kind of identity politics? Do you find that there's kind of a pendulum that shifts away that, oh, it's actually it's a bit sensitive now to talk about women's health. Or are we and I hope you're going to say, no, that's not the case, people are grown up and sensible and we talk about this normally. Is there a political aspect to this? What I would say is, for too long, I mean, you were talking about doctors, right? And the biases they may bring to the table. It is not surprising because medical schools were not teaching sex-based differences, right? We just, again, we were not asking the question. What you're finding now increasingly is a recognition that women are not just small men, right? For too long, when we thought about it is we have to adjust the dose because women on average are physically smaller than men and that takes care of it. I think fortunately, what we're seeing now is that people are understanding we're talking science. We're talking human biology. Women are different from men not only in terms of their reproductive organs, but it's genetics, right? It's hormones. It's so many more variables and it's actually really fascinating scientific questions that are getting asked now. Why is it that women are more likely to suffer dementias, right? And when you think about what's going to be a massive cost burden to society, dementia is very high on that list. It is hard to imagine that we're going to solve and think about the cure for dementia without taking into account the understanding of sex-based differences. With good science, we feel good clinical care that takes us to better health outcomes and stronger economies. People can find that report. Links to it are in the show notes to this episode. Whether it's the next conversation you're having with your doctor, where you're asking about those sex-based differences, or the ways where you think you want to contribute economically or in other ways, to advancing the science and the innovation that will help us close the women's health gap, I would just encourage all our listeners to take action. How Is AI Changing the CFO Office? | RAMP x DFCG | RAISE Summit 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·14:13·8 views·2026-07-23
Jacob WallembergExecutive, RAMP Armand AngeliVP of Transformation & Co-Chair, DFCG
TL;DRJacob Wallenberg of Ramp details the finance automation platform's expansion into Europe and its AI-native tools for streamlining corporate spend, tracking LLM token costs, and automating month-end book closures.
Background on Ramp: Founded in the U.S. 6–7 years ago as a...

TL;DR

Jacob Wallenberg of Ramp details the finance automation platform's expansion into Europe and its AI-native tools for streamlining corporate spend, tracking LLM token costs, and automating month-end book closures.

Company Overview & European Expansion

  • Background on Ramp:
    • Founded in the U.S. 6–7 years ago as a finance automation platform starting with a Visa corporate card
    • Expanded to offer employee reimbursements, procurement software, a travel portal, and banking-like products
    • Serves ~70,000 customers in the U.S., ranging from potato farms in Idaho to Uber and Visa
  • Global Pull: Driven by existing global customers with European footprints (e.g., Stripe, 11 Labs, Cognition) requesting functionality in locations like the UK, Singapore, and Paris
  • Regional Compliance & Sovereignty:
    • Licensed in both Sweden and the UK to ensure regulatory compliance regarding data residency and storage preferences
    • Addresses customer preferences regarding data location and model deployment sovereignty

AI-Native Products in Finance

  • Stack (Book-Closing Agent): Purpose-built automatic agent launched ~1 month prior that handles the entire month-end book-closing process for accountants
  • Policy Agent: LLM-driven tool that evaluates company expenses against policies written in natural language (English, French, etc.) to determine policy compliance
  • AP Fraud Prevention & Extraction: Automated receipt data extraction and fraud detection on invoices
  • Adoption Metrics: ~50% of customers use at least 2 product lines; ~33% use at least 3; setup and results can be realized within 1–2 weeks depending on company size

AI Token Spend Management

  • Tracking & Control: New functionality (entering general availability soon) allowing finance teams to track who across the company is spending tokens and which models they are using
  • Cross-Department Collaboration: Bridges the gap between the CFO and the CIO/CTO to ensure capital and resources are deployed on appropriate models rather than wasting budgets on expensive proprietary tools where open-source options suffice

Integration vs. Replacement Strategy

  • System of Action vs. Record: Ramp positions itself as a "system of action" (deploying, managing, and controlling spend) rather than an ERP or accounting system of record
  • ERP Partnerships: Integrates closely with NetSuite, QuickBooks, Xero, and newer players like Rill and Campfire rather than attempting to replace SAP or other major ERPs

President Trump Makes an Announcement on the Ratepayer Protection Pledge

The White House(3.1M)·33:40·29.2K views·2026-07-23
Donald TrumpPresident, United States
TL;DRPresident Trump announced that over 220 entities have signed the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, requiring major tech companies and AI data centers to fund or build their own energy infrastructure to prevent consumer rate hikes while keeping the U.S.
ahead in AI and manufacturing. ...

TL;DR

President Trump announced that over 220 entities have signed the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, requiring major tech companies and AI data centers to fund or build their own energy infrastructure to prevent consumer rate hikes while keeping the U.S. ahead in AI and manufacturing.

Ratepayer Protection Pledge and Energy Infrastructure

  • Self-Funded Tech Infrastructure:
    • Large tech companies and AI data centers are formally committing to fund or build all required energy infrastructure rather than passing costs to consumers.
    • AI data centers and big tech are essentially becoming their own utilities and building dedicated power plants because current electricity demand is more than double current production levels.
  • Pledge Adoption and Rate Protections:
    • Over 220 utilities, tech companies, state governments, and partners have signed the Ratepayer Protection Pledge since its introduction a few months ago.
    • Electricity rates for 80% of power distributed in America are currently kept in check by the plan.
  • Specific Utility and State Impacts:
    • Georgia Power: Froze base rates for households until 2029; revenue from Google's data center construction is allowing the elimination of property taxes entirely (noted by Gov. Brian Kemp).
    • American Electric Power (AEP): Projects $16 billion in cost offsets for consumers through data center contracts.
    • Alliant Energy (Iowa): Data center deals allow them to freeze electric rates for consumers for at least 5 years.
    • Meta Project (Richland Parish, Louisiana): A $27 billion project provides public school teachers with up to $50,000 in bonuses this year and saves local residents $2.6 billion on electricity bills (noted by Sheldon Jones).

Economic Boom, Investments, and Trade

  • Foreign and Domestic Investment Metrics:
    • $19.2 trillion in investments have poured into the U.S. in the last 12 months, creating hundreds of thousands of jobs (compared to less than $1 trillion in 4 years under the previous administration).
  • Automotive and Manufacturing Growth:
    • Toyota is moving its operations from Mexico to the U.S.
    • The U.S. is building more car plants currently than at any time in history following years of zero new plant construction.
  • Semiconductor Market Progress:
    • The U.S. is projected to capture about 50% of the chip market by the end of the current term, up from virtually none (excluding Intel) at the start of the initiative.
    • An investment in 10% of Intel yielded approximately a $72 billion profit, with Intel's stock price having gone up "many times."

Artificial Intelligence and Global Competition

  • AI Dominance Goals:
    • President Trump stated that AI is bigger than the internet or anything seen thus far, and whoever wins the AI race wins overall.
    • The U.S. is currently leading China and all other countries by a lot in AI and crypto.
  • International Dynamics:
    • Chinese President Xi Jinping is scheduled to visit Washington on September 24th to discuss trade and technology.
    • Foreign nations and lobbyists are spending money on propaganda and PR to convince communities to reject data centers and slow down U.S. progress.

Military, Foreign Policy, and Current Operations

  • Iran Conflict Status:
    • U.S. military operations against Iran have knocked out their navy, air force, radar, and manufacturing capability.
    • Iranian drone capacity is down 84% and missile capacity is down 91%; all 159 ships and 212 airplanes have been neutralized.
  • Venezuela Operations:
    • Operations in Venezuela were completed in a "one-day war" at a profit where the U.S. is taking millions of barrels of oil to Houston and Louisiana, paying for the conflict many times over while Venezuelan oil companies generate record earnings.
  • Casualties:
    • Four American service members were recently killed, bringing total recent casualties across two conflicts to 18.

Key Attendees and Administration Recognition

  • Administration Officials: Secretary of Energy Dan Brouillette, Energy/Venezuela lead Chris Wright, and EPA/Permitting official Lee Zeldin (praised for approving projects in 2-4 weeks instead of years).
  • Governors in Attendance: Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry, Nebraska Governor Jim Pillen, Idaho Governor Brad Little, and Georgia Governor Brian Kemp.
  • Industry Executives and Community Leaders: Entergy CEO Drew Marsh, Southern Company CEO Chris Womack, Meta President Dena Powell, Columbia County Commission Chairman Doug Duncan, and Richland Parish School District's Sheldon Jones.

IBM CEO: 'Technology Spend Will Keep Growing'

Bloomberg Tech(733.0K)·15:09·351 views·2026-07-23
Arvind KrishnaCEO, IBM Ed LudlowHost, Bloomberg Technology Romaine BostickHost, Bloomberg Technology
TL;DRIBM CEO Arvind Krishna addressed the company's lowered full-year sales forecast—driven by a temporary CapEx slowdown and mainframe sales dip among Fortune 100 clients—while reaffirming free cash flow targets and highlighting strong enterprise demand for AI, Red Hat software, and quantum computing.

TL;DR

IBM CEO Arvind Krishna addressed the company's lowered full-year sales forecast—driven by a temporary CapEx slowdown and mainframe sales dip among Fortune 100 clients—while reaffirming free cash flow targets and highlighting strong enterprise demand for AI, Red Hat software, and quantum computing.

Mainframe Performance and Capacity Growth

  • Z17 Capacity Surge: The current Z17 machine, launched in May 2025, has achieved 130% capacity growth compared to the prior generation.
  • Client Adoption: 85% of mainframe clients are actively increasing their capacity, while 15% are optimizing.
  • Structural Value Proposition: Addressing investor comparisons to the 1990s mainframe shift, Krishna noted that unit costs for protected, resilient, and burst-capacity workloads on the mainframe remain 5 to 15 times cheaper than alternatives, making it an architecturally superior platform rather than a displaced technology.

Financial Guidance, Software, and Cost Management

  • Full-Year Revenue Guidance: IBM narrowed its full-year sales forecast to 4% to 5% growth, depending on whether CapEx headwinds persist through September.
  • Software Portfolio Split: 80% of the software business operates as an annuity, consumption, and OPEX-based model growing at approximately 8%, featuring products like Red Hat, Confluent, and HashiCorp. The remaining 20% is CapEx-sensitive.
  • Cash Flow and EBITDA: Over the past four years, cash flow growth has outpaced revenue at 7% to 8% to 9% annually (driven by adjusted EBITDA), supported by productivity levers and maintaining the dividend.
  • Supply Chain Backlog: IBM exited the second quarter with half a billion dollars of backlog in distributed infrastructure, storage, and Unix systems due to component demand.
  • Third-Party Spend: Headcount has remained flat over recent years; additional efficiencies are being targeted via third-party spend rather than widespread workforce reductions.
  • Starbucks Client Relationship: Regarding Starbucks replacing an aging 10-year-old Tririga real estate lease management tool ($2M/year client), Krishna noted that interaction-based software is easily displaced by AI agents, but expects Starbucks to remain a larger overall client next year via other IBM portfolios like HashiCorp and security.

Macroeconomic Environment and Component Pricing

  • Global GDP and Tech Spending: IBM relies on a projected global GDP growth rate of 2% to 3% for the year, with technology spending tracking 3 to 4 points higher (putting the overall tech market at 5% to 6% to 7% growth). Consulting is expected to grow at 1% to 2% to 3%.
  • Underlying Component Inflation: Over the past 18 months, semiconductor memory pricing is up 3 to 4 times, networking infrastructure is up 60% to 80%, and fiber and connectors have also risen significantly.

Talent and Hiring

  • College Hiring Surge: IBM hired 3 times as many college graduates this year compared to last year.
  • Talent Pool: The applicant database contains roughly 20 million resumes, shifting the internal focus from talent acquisition to career pathing and ensuring employees effectively utilize AI productivity tools.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Enterprise Tech Spend Share (Arvind Krishna): Technology spend will grow from historical averages of 3% to 5% to 6% on average today, and will reach 10% of every enterprise's budget by 2035.
  • Quantum Computing Timeline & Scale (Arvind Krishna): Quantum computing has transitioned from science to engineering; IBM will deliver a large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum computer capable of 100 million computations by 2029.
  • Quantum Total Addressable Market (Arvind Krishna): By the end of the 2030s, quantum computing will create a $1 trillion total market opportunity in terms of economic value.

Notable Quotes

"If there is a cheaper alternate for that workload, I'll sort of look at you and say that means in 5 to 15 years it will move off. But that's not the case. Right now for the workloads that are on, we can show the clients that it's 5 to 15 times cheaper to keep it on the mainframe than not." — Arvind Krishna, CEO of IBM
"I think technology spend is going to become a larger and larger part of every enterprise's budget... I will not be surprised if by 2035, it's 10% of everyone's budget." — Arvind Krishna, CEO of IBM
"When things are 2 years away, I wouldn't call them a moonshot. I think quantum is now in the engineering realm as opposed to the science realm for the next 5 years." — Arvind Krishna, CEO of IBM
Shares by IBM are trading near their lowest levels since November 24, September of 2024. The company dialed back its full-year sales forecast after a pretty steep drop in mainframe sales which weighed on results. Joining us from New York is the co-host of Bloomberg's The Close, Romaine Bostick, alongside IBM CEO, Arvind Krishna. Romaine, you've seen the reaction amongst investors here. Some concerns here about that lowered sales forecast overall, as well as softness in software. You've characterized this as basically a shortfall, for one quarter, that is limited to CapEx sensitive areas of the portfolio, but that's still a meaningful area of your portfolio. Where sales coming into that quarter, was that pipeline overstated? I don't believe so, because when we look at all the deals that didn't close, I think we have done enough verification, including with the clients, to know that they were very real and it was a reprioritization of the CapEx spend at the end of the quarter. This was pretty confined to call it the Fortune 100, the deals that were within a subset of those. Now, one-third of what didn't happen has already come back. So that tells us that this was a reprioritization and those deals were very real as opposed to us being optimistic in our projections. And Romaine, I would also add, I think that maintaining our free cash flow tells us that we have levers on productivity and conviction and confidence in the business, and that is also I think going to serve our investors well. But the next few months will tell us that. Well, on that cash flow figure, yes, and that certainly pleased a lot of analysts and investors out there. That billion dollar number of projected free cash flow growth, you maintaining the dividend as well, but you've largely done that so far by cutting costs. So that raises the question that if we are anticipating slower growth on the revenue side, does that mean more cost cuts are in store? Delivering on the Promise of AI Through Trusted Partnerships | Simon Ahdoot, Hypertec | RAISE 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·13:30·1 views·2026-07-23
Simon AhdootCEO, Hypertec
TL;DRAI represents the largest civilizational infrastructure expenditure in history, requiring a decade-scale acceleration that no single institution can build alone; success depends entirely on multi-stakeholder trusted partnerships spanning compute, power, land, capital, and regional regulation.

TL;DR

AI represents the largest civilizational infrastructure expenditure in history, requiring a decade-scale acceleration that no single institution can build alone; success depends entirely on multi-stakeholder trusted partnerships spanning compute, power, land, capital, and regional regulation.

The Scale and Opportunity of AI Infrastructure

  • Historical Comparison: Hypertec has operated for 42 years—spanning PCs, client-server architecture, the internet, and cloud computing—but CEO Simon Ahdoot asserts that all prior IT revolutions pale in comparison to AI.
  • Societal Impact Sectors:
    • Healthcare: Currently characterized as too expensive, too slow, and lacking proper visibility and results, making it ripe for AI-driven transformation.
    • Power Grid Allocation: Current peak-to-available power generation gaps run as high as 40% due to inefficient allocation; smarter distribution can yield massive systemic gains.
    • Construction: Economic output and daily workflows suffer from persistent inefficiencies (exemplified by Montreal's endless road construction); better coordination via technology can boost productivity.
  • Unprecedented Expenditure: AI is slated to become the largest civilizational expenditure in human history, compressed into a single decade rather than the 50 to 60 years it took for electrification.

Core Deployment Challenges

  • The Small Business Implementation Gap: While large construction firms and enterprises can deploy AI stacks, the vast majority of companies have under five employees and lack dedicated IT staff, making full workflow integration extremely difficult beyond basic research queries.
  • Infrastructure Constraints:
    • Power: The industry is witnessing multiple gigawatt-scale power projects unlike anything seen previously in IT.
    • Talent: Sourcing skilled technical talent is exceptionally difficult, compounded by the monumental challenge of retraining experienced professionals to work in entirely new ways (e.g., teaching doctors with decades of practice to adopt AI-assisted workflows).
  • Public Pushback and Environmental Concerns: Data centers face growing community opposition over high water consumption and local heat island effects (such as measured temperature increases around Arizona data centers).
  • Regional Regulatory Divergence: Operating globally means navigating starkly different regional regulations, cultural norms, and legal frameworks where methods permitted in one jurisdiction fail in another.

The Necessity of Ecosystem Partnerships

  • The Multi-Disciplinary Stack: Building a single AI project requires an intricate assembly of distinct providers:
    • Compute & Equipment: Providers like Nvidia, Google, and AMD.
    • Real Estate & Facilities: Land acquisition, data center design, construction, and ongoing facility management.
    • Infrastructure Operations: GPU cluster management and IT infrastructure oversight (e.g., Bazz HPC).
    • Software & Models: Enterprise AI model developers (e.g., Cohere).
    • Connectivity & Capital: Enterprise networking providers, massive capital backing, and credit rating partners for long-term contract viability.
  • Hyperscaler Limitations: Even tech hyperscalers with massive cash reserves cannot shoulder the entire capital and operational burden directly out of pocket and must rely on partner ecosystems.

Sovereign AI and Cross-Generational Collaboration

  • The Sovereign AI Imperative: Regional partnerships are mandatory to navigate local regulations, public trust, and physical deployments, making Sovereign AI a critical framework in markets like Canada.
  • Case Study — The Canadian Four-Way Partnership:
    • Hypertec: 42 years old (founded 1984), providing compute and IT infrastructure.
    • Bell Canada (Bell AI Fabric): Founded in 1880 (13 years after Canadian confederation), bringing deep institutional background and long-standing operational practices.
    • Bazz HPC: Founded in 2017 (9 years old), serving as the infrastructure operator running the GPU stack.
    • Cohere: Founded in 2019 (7 years old), supplying enterprise AI models.
  • Bridging Institutional Divorces: The primary strategic challenge for legacy institutions (like a 140-year-old telecom) is learning to pivot and become flexible, while newer technology firms must learn how to respect and leverage centuries of institutional knowledge.
"I heard a talk recently where somebody pointed out that artificial intelligence may be the single largest civilizational expenditure in human history... We're trying to get there within a decade." — Simon Ahdoot
"Imagine, you have a 7-year-old, a 9-year-old, a 42-year-old, and a 140-year-old all working together. We were able to make that happen, which enables this incredible project." — Simon Ahdoot
"If you want to be able to succeed within AI, what we're going to need to be able to do, if we want to deliver on that promise, is we need to identify the right partners, build up that trust, and get to where we can make amazing things happen together." — Simon Ahdoot
Good afternoon, everybody. Thank you for joining. At Hypertec, we've been around for 42 years, so in the space of IT, that's roughly 100,000 years, something in that range. A long, long time, and in all that time, we've seen a lot of amazing things get developed, get deployed, and get implemented. I remember when we started—I wasn't the CEO at the time, just to be clear—back in 1984. It was the personal computer. An amazing revolution. Then it went to the server-based, the client-server architecture, as I'm sure some of you may remember. It went to the cloud, the development of the internet, then we went on to the high-performance systems that were able to do amazing things. All of a sudden, we started seeing the cooling was an issue, that we needed to figure out a way to make things more distributed, the apps, all these things. They've had an enormous impact on our lives; they've changed the way that we live and the way we do business. But I can say with full confidence that all that pales in comparison to what I'm seeing is possible with AI today. Now, what are those possibilities we're talking about? What are those opportunities we have not just directing business, but overall? I think of the challenges we have in the wide world today. Just for fun, once in a while when I talk to people, I can, with high confidence, assert that healthcare is not what it should be. I don't even know where you come from, but I can tell you healthcare is either too expensive or too slow, and maybe both. It doesn't deliver the kind of results you want. The visibility isn't where it should be, and that's a real missed opportunity. We could do a lot better, and the way we could do that, for instance, is with AI. I think of, for example, power. When we look at those events we've had where the power went out, I learned that the difference between the power used and the power peak, the power available, could be as much as 40% just based on allocation. They need to make it available everywhere, but it is not always used everywhere. So if you could get the power to the right place at the right time, all of a sudden you make a huge difference. Then you have construction. I'm from Montreal; our main icon is the orange cone for all the construction that's always happening all over the roads, and I can say it's a big distraction. It's a big slowdown for everything we try to do on a daily basis. If we could make sure that they could do the work right the first time and get the coordination down pat, that would make all of our lives easier, boost economic output, and have a really great impact. I don't need to sell you guys on what AI can do, but it's an enormous historical opportunity. Now, okay, so let's do it, right? There are some challenges in the mix. Some really big challenges. I heard a talk recently where somebody pointed out that artificial intelligence may be the single largest civilizational expenditure in human history. The thing is, if you look at the full scale, we're talking about all the infrastructure and all the development, we're not doing it over the course of, let's say, 50 or 60 years like we did for electrification. ChatGPT went public for individual use three and a half years ago. We're trying to get there within a decade. We're talking about an unprecedented acceleration and speed of public works. And that's just for the base of it; we still need to figure out how to use it. I had a discussion recently where we were talking to people in the construction industry, and they were saying how the big firms can get it going, but the majority of companies are under five employees. How do you get an AI stack deployed for less than five people? Yes, they'll be able to go on ChatGPT, try to figure out some research, or maybe go on Claude to get a contract reviewed, but that's not what we're talking about. We're talking about the full implementation to speed up their workflows, to interconnect their processes, and to make sure that they can coordinate in a way that human beings just can't manage. How do you get that done if you're under five people? They don't have an IT guy. That represents one of the big challenges: not only building up that infrastructure, but figuring out how to deploy it. When we look at those challenges, I've brought up the infrastructure. One of the big things in infrastructure is the power. Guys, when's the last time you saw multiple gigawatt projects of power in anything other than AI? I've never seen it in my lifetime; it wasn't a thing. We talked about the talent. There are talented individuals who can do this. Again, all of you who are in leadership positions, I'm sure you struggled to find that talent. It's not easy, but it's also about training the people to work differently. Training is hard. Figuring out how to take somebody who's really good at what they do and teaching them to do that same kind of thing in a completely different way is a monumental challenge throughout civilization. And that's for those of us in tech. Imagine for a doctor's clinic; try to get a doctor who's been practicing for 30 years and explain to him that there's a much better way to do what he's doing. It's going to be a huge challenge that we need to get through. Then there's going to be the public trust. The US has been the biggest market for everything to do with the data center, but what we're seeing with data centers and AI is a lot of public pushback. They're concerned around water consumption and the impact on their communities. I even saw an article down in Arizona where they were measuring that the temperature goes up around a data center for AI. The one thing they don't need, and the one thing we don't need right now, is more heat. If that becomes a concern, if that's not addressed, that's going to be a big issue. Then you have what comes along with that, and what's very prevalent here in Europe, which is the regulation. As many of us in business, we do business all over the world with Hypertec. One thing we can tell you, and I'm sure a lot of you know, just because you can do a certain thing a certain way in one country doesn't mean that works elsewhere. The regulations in different areas are very different. Figuring out how to work well in a given jurisdiction, in a given territory, really depends on the regional regulations, the regional norms, and the regional culture. How do we solve this? How do we solve this multi-dimensional puzzle and really realize all those opportunities and all that potential that we have with AI? The fact of the matter is, and something I've been observing a lot in the AI space, which is very encouraging, you have to work with trusted partners. You have to find the right people to work with, find people where you can build up a trusting relationship, where you can work together to get a better outcome than what you could do alone. At Hypertec, we do the compute, the IT part, but we also do all the way down to data center construction. We do the development and the design, and one thing I find remarkable is how many different things need to come together for one project to go forward. You need to get the actual equipment put together—that's the compute. Nvidia is often the biggest one on this, but you also have Google and AMD who are pushing for this. You need to have the land, you need to have the power, and you need to either have or build a facility. These are all different providers. You need to figure out the networking; we need to get everything networked together. You need somebody to manage the cluster on an operating perspective. You need somebody for the AI model and the AI applications. You'll need somebody to manage the data center, somebody to manage the overall computing infrastructure, and, one of the really big pieces here, you'll need an enormous amount of capital and potentially even a backer for the credit rating on the long-term viability of the contracts. This is a lot of different people that need to come together to be able to make it happen. How many single institutions can do this? One of the things I found striking is when you look at the hyperscalers—those are the ones that come to mind—they have the biggest part of this stack. But even for them, with that cumulative wealth, all that cash we keep hearing about on their balance sheets, they can't afford to push this very far direct out of pocket. They need to work with other firms, they need to work with partners to be able to get this going. Nobody can do this alone. In terms of those partnerships, to be able to succeed in deploying AI, finding the right partnerships and finding those in the different territories is key. Sovereign AI has been a big topic lately because it addresses some of those pieces. You have to make sure you cover the regulations, and make sure you're able to deploy the physical reality and make it happen. You're not going to be able to succeed with that if you don't have regional partners. Sovereign AI has been a big topic in Canada, and we had an announcement recently that really shows an example of where multiple different firms can come together and make a project happen by working together. We had an announcement between Hypertec, Bell—AI Fabric in Canada—and for those of you not Canadian, it's a very well-established company over time. Cohere, an AI model company, developed their model and they are very good at enterprise AI, and Bazz HPC is an infrastructure operator. They're the ones who are able to run the GPU stack to be able to help it function well for their client. Our four organizations came together to get this project going. I want to highlight something that really points out some of the challenges you're going to encounter and the importance of how to position for these partnerships. I mentioned that at Hypertec we've been in business for 42 years. Again, in the AI space, that's quite a long time. But Bell Canada was founded in 1880. Now, I know that for Europe, 1880 is not a big deal; I saw a hat maker that was founded in the 1700s. But I can tell you, in North America, it's very rare. It turns out that Bell Canada is only a few years younger than Canada. So Canada was 1867. Fast forward 13 years, you've got Bell Canada. That's the scope. It's almost the age of the country. With that, you have a lot of institutional background, a lot of institutional knowledge, and a lot of practices that have been in place for more than 100 years. They're going on 150 years soon. The challenge for them, those established players, is to figure out how to become flexible. The challenge for us is to figure out how to work with them. What can they do well? What can we do well? How can we make things easier for them? How can they adapt to be able to participate in this new movement, especially when you're talking about Europe? There are going to be a lot of very well-established, very well-entrenched firms that we need to figure out how to work with. Working through that partnership, working through building that trust, working through acknowledging their strengths, benefiting from their strengths, but also acknowledging where they may not be able to pivot so quickly—that is the work. Imagine, I was talking about a doctor who's been doing something for 30 years; what if you have an institution that's been successful for a century or two centuries because of how they've been doing things? How do you convince them to do things differently? You need to think about that. To round up the crew, we've got Bazz HPC, they've been around since 2017—that's nine years—and then the baby of the group, Cohere, since 2019, seven years. Imagine, you have a 7-year-old, a 9-year-old, a 42-year-old, and a 140-year-old all working together. We were able to make that happen, which enables this incredible project that lets us build up those capabilities within Canada so that we can become a participant in the AI space. Overall, if you want to be able to succeed within AI, what we're going to need to do, if we want to deliver on that promise, is identify the right partners, build up that trust, and get to where we can make amazing things happen together. The pie that we have here, the scope of the opportunity, is literally unprecedented. Not just an opportunity for economic benefit for those who are participating, but for the entire community, the entire society around us. It's up to us to figure out how to work together, how to work collaboratively, how to work in partnership—being good partners—to be able to help realize all the benefits that this can bring us. I want to thank you guys for taking the time to be with us. Have a wonderful rest of the day and I hope you all stay cool.

Elon Musk on AI: humans will no longer be in control in ten years | The Economist

The Economist(4.0M)·10:36·474.0K views·2026-07-23
Elon MuskCEO, Tesla and SpaceX Zanny Minton BeddoesEditor-in-chief, The Economist
TL;DR"I think AI may exceed the sum of human intelligence in about in around 5…
Elon Musk predicts that artificial intelligence will exceed total human intelligence in approximately five years and leave humans out of control within a decade, steering humanity toward an inevitable "age of amazing abundance." To mitigate the residual 10–20% existential...

TL;DR: Elon Musk predicts that artificial intelligence will exceed total human intelligence in approximately five years and leave humans out of control within a decade, steering humanity toward an inevitable "age of amazing abundance." To mitigate the residual 10–20% existential risk, he proposes a peer-review regulatory model where rival AI labs audit each other’s frontier models before government intervention.

AI Timelines and the Future of Human Control

  • Intelligence Horizon: Elon Musk estimates that AI will exceed the sum of human intelligence in roughly 5 years, noting that there will be virtually nothing AI cannot do better than humans, aside from being human.
  • Loss of Human Control: Elon Musk asserts it is unlikely humans will be in control in 10 years, comparing the dynamic to the intelligence gap between humans and chimpanzees—noting humans are essentially evolved chimpanzees who still eat bananas.
  • The "Age of Abundance": Elon Musk predicts the most likely outcome of this technological shift is an age of amazing abundance where anyone can have anything they can think of, barring a massive global thermonuclear war (World War III).
  • Inevitable Momentum: Elon Musk states there is no way to stop the incredible momentum of AI and robotics, adding that even if a stop button existed, humans probably shouldn't press it because the destination is overall positive.

Existential Risk and Philosophical Outlook

  • P-Doom Estimate: Elon Musk maintains there is still a 10 to 20% chance that killer robots and AI could mean the end of humanity.
  • Philosophical Shift: Elon Musk explains that his current philosophy is to look on the bright side and "enjoy the ride," noting that in the long run, humanity faces the eventual heat death of the universe under standard physics anyway.
  • Historical Accelerants: Elon Musk reflects that his past efforts—creating OpenAI as a counterweight to Google's monopoly and the subsequent spin-out of Anthropic—ironically resulted in knock-on effects that accelerated AI development further, leading him to conclude "all roads lead to acceleration."

Industry Self-Regulation and Peer Review

  • Competitor Cross-Auditing: Elon Musk recommends that the most immediate safety measure is for leading AI companies to meet bi-weekly and give competitors a 1-to-2-week early access window to review new frontier models.
  • Industry Incentives: Elon Musk argues that rival AI companies have a natural incentive to keep each other honest by highlighting security risks and advocating for delayed releases of competitors' flawed models.
  • Government Threshold: Elon Musk explains that technical frontier insights are difficult for non-technical government officials to judge, meaning industry peers should first flag risks, and governments should only step in if a company fails to address a flagged risk.
  • Urgency and Meeting Pace: Elon Musk notes that with AI breakthrough announcements occurring multiple times per week, safety coordination mechanisms need to be established immediately rather than over a standard 6-month timeline.

Personal Dynamics Among AI Lab Leaders

  • Critique of Sam Altman: Elon Musk criticizes Sam Altman for transforming OpenAI from a non-profit, open-source company intended to be owned by the world into an $800 billion for-profit, closed-source entity.
  • Anthropic's Origins: Elon Musk notes that Dario Amodei and the Anthropic team left OpenAI specifically because they did not trust Sam Altman.
  • Assessment of Anthropic: Elon Musk describes Dario Amodei as a very principled person who cares about the world's future, adding that no one he has met at Anthropic sets off his "evil detector" and that they appear well-intentioned.
  • Putting Differences Aside: Elon Musk affirms that despite personal vitriol and disagreements, industry leaders can and will set aside their personal differences for the good of the world when necessary.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Elon Musk predicts that AI will exceed the sum of human intelligence in roughly 5 years.
  • Elon Musk predicts that humans will no longer be in control of AI in 10 years.
  • Elon Musk predicts the most likely outcome of advanced AI will be an "age of amazing abundance" by 2036.
  • Elon Musk estimates a 10 to 20% chance that killer robots and AI could result in the end of humanity in the long run.

Benchmarks vs. Reality: Lessons from 750 Trillion Tokens | Chris Clark, OpenRouter | RAISE 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·19:32·33 views·2026-07-23
Chris ClarkCo-Founder & COO, OpenRouter
TL;DREmpirical real-world usage data processed across trillions of tokens reveals a stark gap between theoretical model benchmarks and actual production performance, showing that infrastructure reality—such as caching efficiencies, token chattiness, and…
serving provider...

TL;DR

Empirical real-world usage data processed across trillions of tokens reveals a stark gap between theoretical model benchmarks and actual production performance, showing that infrastructure reality—such as caching efficiencies, token chattiness, and serving provider bugs—dramatically alters costs and success rates.

Multi-Model Reality & Adoption Trends

  • Growing Inference Costs: Inference is rapidly becoming the second-largest operating expense for knowledge companies; while past growth was driven by AI-native companies treating tokens as COGS, 2026 has seen tokens increasingly used directly as headcount replacement and operating expense.
  • The Multi-Model Shift: Despite periodic market consolidation fears, the AI ecosystem is definitively multi-model, with companies leveraging diverse models based on specific agentic or operational needs.
  • Open-Weight vs. Frontier Gap: Open-weight models (primarily from Chinese labs) have maintained a remarkably persistent 4-to-6-month lag behind US frontier labs rather than closing the gap via recursive self-improvement.
  • Regional Adoption Variations: Global regions display distinct adoption curves for open-weight models, influenced by local marginal costs and declining confidence in single-region providers (such as the market removal of Fable).

Agentic Workloads & The "Glass Slipper" Effect

  • Challenging Cut-Overs: Transitioning agentic workloads between models is increasingly difficult because models like DeepSeek 4 require different prompting techniques (e.g., specific behavioral feedback) rather than drop-in replacement.
  • Jagged Frontier Intelligence: Models scoring higher on theoretical intelligence benchmarks (e.g., Kimmy K26) do not automatically see higher adoption than models optimized specifically for long-horizon agentic workflows (e.g., DeepSeek 4).
  • The Glass Slipper Effect: Initial cohort users of a model who successfully deploy an agent tend to stick with that specific model long-term ("the shoe fits") and freeze at that point of intelligence rather than constantly migrating to newer models.
  • Two Agent Paradigms: Builders must choose between two strategies:
    • *Stable Agents:* Workloads where a model is proven to work in production, shifting focus to reliability and cost reduction.
    • *Frontier-Chasing Agents:* Workloads where the product improves with every frontier release, requiring caution against premature cost optimization that risks falling behind competitors.

Pricing Realities & Serving Provider Variance

  • Theoretical vs. Realized Pricing: Static price tables fail to reflect production costs; real-world factors like cache hit rates and output token chattiness cause actual price ratios between models (e.g., GLM 52 vs. GPT 55) to diverge significantly from initial paper calculations.
  • The "Pile of Math" Engineering Gap: Identical model weights yield vastly different production success rates depending on the provider's serving infrastructure, API configuration, and parsing logic.
  • Tool Call Reliability: Tool call failures are driven by prosaic engineering issues—such as regular expression bugs parsing chat templates, hallucinated tool names, invalid JSON output, or mismatched parameters—rather than adversarial model degradation.

Notable Quotes

"The empirical usage of models is the ultimate un-gameable benchmark. Like, where are you putting your dollars? What are you using them for? And that is a very hard benchmark to game." — Chris Clark
"The frontier is jagged... a benchmark that simply says that one of these models is more intelligent than another, but it takes us a little while to learn these things and it takes our customers a little while to discover it and actually implement them." — Chris Clark
"Sometimes different providers have a bug in their regular expression and don't parse the chat templates correctly. And now all of a sudden, tool calls fail under certain circumstances... they have nothing to do with the model itself." — Chris Clark

The Trillion Dollar Question: Financing AI's Infrastructure | Modular, IREN & More | RAISE 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·38:59·110 views·2026-07-23
Tim DavisRepresentative, Modular Kent DraperRepresentative, IREN Jeremie Eliahou OntiverosModerator, SemiAnalysis
TL;DRAI infrastructure is experiencing an unprecedented capital-intensive buildout driven by massive demand-supply imbalances, requiring creative financing structures like investment-grade debt, asset-backed leases, prepayments, and behind-the-meter nuclear power uprates.
To de-risk...

TL;DR

AI infrastructure is experiencing an unprecedented capital-intensive buildout driven by massive demand-supply imbalances, requiring creative financing structures like investment-grade debt, asset-backed leases, prepayments, and behind-the-meter nuclear power uprates. To de-risk this trillions-dollar investment, the industry is increasingly leveraging vertical integration, software-enabled compute fungibility, and multi-tenant platforms to balance long-term infrastructure supply with short-term token demand.

Panelists & Participants

  • Moderator: Jeremie Eliahou Ontiveros (SemiAnalysis)
  • Tim Davis: Co-founder, Modular (recently acquired by Qualcomm; building a unified hardware abstraction layer across silicon)
  • Kent Draper: Chief Commercial Officer, IREN (vertically integrated AI cloud service owning GPUs and data centers)
  • Erwan Menard: Crusoe (vertically integrated AI factory building gigawatt-scale data center campuses and providing megawatts, GPU hours, and tokens)
  • Wayne Nelms: CTO & Co-founder, Orn (compute exchange aggregating excess compute capacity and serving smaller frontier labs/inference providers)
  • James B. Krellenstein: CEO & Co-founder, Alva Energy (nuclear energy technology provider enabling gigawatt-scale project-financed power uprates)

Financing Structures & Capital Intensity

  • Capital Intensity and Debt Structuring: IREN notes that top-tier GPUs in liquid-cooled data centers require capital commitments of $40 to $50 million per megawatt, and IREN raised $14 billion in the 12 months leading up to May 2026. IREN secured an investment-grade rated debt instrument (blended cost of 3%) for a five-year Microsoft contract for GB200s, funding 96% of the capital commitment via prepayments and debt.
  • Residual Value and Post-Contract Valuation: Lenders are increasingly willing to attribute value to the post-contracted period of data center assets (which have 20+ year useful lives, compared to shorter-lived GPU cycles), allowing providers to borrow significantly more than what is covered by initial contracts.
  • Sub-Investment Grade Counterparties: Capital providers are showing a significantly increased willingness to fund against sub-investment grade counterparties (smaller AI labs and enterprises), opening up broader end-customer bases despite shorter-term token-based models.

Energy Sourcing & Nuclear Solutions

  • Grid Load Growth: Alva Energy highlights that 50% of electric power load growth in the United States is currently coming from data centers, representing a structural shift not seen since Thomas Edison founded the industry 100 years ago.
  • Hyperscaler Nuclear PPAs: Major tech companies have secured large nuclear power purchase agreements:
    • Amazon: Partnered with Talen for the Susquehanna nuclear plant.
    • Microsoft: Committed to buying 800+ megawatts electrical from Three Mile Island Unit 1 for 15+ years at ~$116/MWh tied to CPI.
    • Meta: Procured 440 megawatts electrical of uprates from Vistra through 2035.
    • Google: Bringing back Duane Arnold Unit 1 in Iowa.
  • Project-Financed Nuclear Uprates: Alva Energy’s technology can operate existing pressurized water reactors to add 200 to 300 megawatts electrical per reactor across 41 U.S. sites. Unlike corporate-debt-reliant utility models, Alva's approach achieves 100% firm fixed-price EPC contracts for project financing without relying on utility rate bases, bypassing new licensing regulatory bottlenecks on a 3 to 5 year timeline.

Vertical Integration & Compute Monetization

  • Vertical Integration Strategy: Crusoe builds gigawatt-scale data center campuses (for themselves and hyperscalers like OpenAI and Microsoft) to maintain control over delivery schedules, margins, and reliability.
  • Monetization Tiers: Crusoe monetizes capacity via three distinct models with varying margin profiles:

1. Selling megawatts (data center real estate/power infrastructure).

2. Selling GPU hours (clusters).

3. Selling tokens (managed inference via API endpoints with SLAs).

  • Open-Demand and Pricing Volatility: Orn tracks on-demand compute pricing, noting that H100 pricing dropped to roughly $70 in fall 2025 but rebounded to $2.50.

Software, Fungibility, and Heterogeneous Compute

  • The Software Moat: Modular notes that the primary moat for incumbent silicon (Nvidia/AMD) is software (e.g., CUDA), and debt underwriting today heavily favors Nvidia/AMD due to liquidity and software lock-in.
  • Compute Heterogeneity: Workloads are shifting from raw GPU inference to agentic long-running workloads requiring CPU orchestration, pre-fill/decode disaggregation, and memory scaling (memory prices have 12x'd in the last year).
  • Hardware Abstraction: Modular's software aims to make compute fungible across multiple silicon types (including Qualcomm's platform post-acquisition), enabling data center providers to decouple from single-vendor hardware locks.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Tim Davis (Modular): Software will become the primary mechanism to radically change the underlying financing of the entire industry by driving compute fungibility across diverse silicon.
  • James B. Krellenstein (Alva Energy): Nuclear power uprates will deploy on a concrete timeline of 3 to 5 years, while brand-new nuclear builds will take 10-year (decade) timelines.
