I think one of the biggest challenges, especially when it comes to Neo Clouds, which are these new companies in the space that are deploying billions of capital, is that the industry is getting more and more capital intensive. Even on the utility side, securing power, every single layer is getting more and more capital intensive. It is a crucial question, and we have an amazing panel today from folks at different layers. To get started, it would be great if maybe one by one you could introduce yourselves, your company, and broadly speaking, how you think about financing in terms of business development on your side. Can you be 100% self-funded, or do you actually need to raise a lot and have big offtakes from large hyperscalers? How does it work generally speaking for you guys? Tim, please. Yeah, I'm happy to get started. Hi everyone. My name is Tim. I'm one of the co-founders of a company called Modular. We're building a unified hardware abstraction layer. It is software, all software, but it enables you to run AI models across multiple pieces of silicon. That was the vision that we set out to achieve. We have actually just been acquired by Qualcomm, and so now we're very excited about scaling our platform on a much bigger scale. You asked a fairly loaded first question. One of the things that we strongly believe in is that the moat that exists today for the largest incumbent silicon provider, certainly in the data center, is software. If you are able to change the software infrastructure such that compute becomes more fungible, you change things. Debt underwriting of any GPU today is really only for Nvidia and AMD. Any other piece of silicon in the market is basically financed through equity investments. Our vision has been to build an alternate software platform that enables you to easily execute across different pieces of silicon. In that world, which we are very close to now, scaling very significantly changes the compute dynamics because today, all of the debt-based underwriting on GPUs has a premium for the lock-in that's attached. Any underwriter that wants to underwrite an asset ultimately wants liquidity attached to that asset. Today, if you don't have software that enables you to easily move that asset between different data centers or ultimately resell it to different providers, you have to underprice the asset. We believe that once you get better compute fungibility, you increase the liquidity of compute more broadly. The compute asset continues to be very valuable, but certain GPUs, in our opinion, become less valuable over time. I'll stop talking. Two Australian accents in a row—no one is going to understand what we're saying in here. I am Ken Draper, chief commercial officer of IREN. We are a vertically integrated AI cloud service. We own and operate GPUs, but also the underlying data centers where we're running our GPUs. From a financing perspective, all of that means that we have a very large capital bill, given that we're funding both the data centers and the GPU build-out. To give you an idea, top-tier GPUs at the moment in liquid-cooled data centers require a capital commitment of 40 to 50 million dollars a megawatt, and we're building at gigawatt scale. The numbers add up very quickly. Over the 12 months to May of this year, we raised 1.4 billion dollars, and that is only going to increase from here as we start embarking on a larger scale build-out of liquid-cooled data centers. We utilize a bunch of different financing structures and sources of debt capital, and it is very tied to the type of contracts that we're signing. For example, we signed a five-year contract with Microsoft for GB 200s. That is financed through a GPU-specific debt financing. It was the first or one of the first rated debt instruments in the market. It is the highest-rated debt instrument that we are aware of at an A- investment-grade rating. We're able to fund 96% of the capital commitment for that GPU cluster between a prepayment from Microsoft and the debt financing at a blended cost of 3%. That is a structure that you can utilize when you have highly investment-grade or highly creditworthy offtakers and very strong contracts. In addition to that, we've utilized quite a lot of lease-style financing as well as being heavy issuers in the convertible debt market. We are really looking to match the sources of funding with the various different structures and underlying contracts within our business. Hi, everybody. My name is Erwan. I represent Crusoe, which is another Neo Cloud, just like IREN—an AI factory dedicated to AI workloads. First of all, I think a lot of what you shared is very representative of the creativeness that's going on right now in the market. We are observing similar tools being in play. A couple of things maybe to add and build on your comments: one, we shouldn't lose line of sight of the fact that the demand is overwhelming. A lot of us have been in the industry for a while, and the current state of imbalance between demand and supply is also part of this financing equation. Another aspect is how do you monetize those assets? At Crusoe, we build gigawatt-scale data center campuses for ourselves but also for hyperscalers. We've done it for OpenAI, and we're doing it for Microsoft. From a company structure point of view, that means a flow of lease-guaranteed cash injection, and then you can finance the corresponding assets over the lifetime of that long contract. The next thing you can get from us are GPU clusters, and we use tools like what was just described. The third way is tokens. Having built an Inference Stack, being able to do managed inference with your favorite open model that you may tune on our cloud or a model you've built by yourself—and you don't want to deal with the infrastructure underneath but want us to serve it back to you as an API endpoint with an SLA—there, you're selling tokens. You have different ways to monetize the megawatt, and you can align your financing strategies accordingly. Needless to say, those three different models have different margin profiles as well. You have the offtakers, the creativity in the new tools, and the different ways to monetize the megawatt; all of these ingredients are part of cooking the financing recipe. Hi everyone. I'm Wayne. I'm the CTO and co-founder of Orn, based in New York. I think we're a little bit different than the previous two speakers. We are a compute exchange. We actually aggregate supply of excess compute capacity and find offtake for that capacity on our platform. It is a fully end-to-end managed service offering Kubernetes, Slurm, VMs, everything included. I think the way we see financing is a little bit different, considering we're kind of the new kids on the block. These days, everyone is relying on creditworthy offtake. To finance a new data center, you need to show your lender that you have someone on the end of the line that can pay for all the compute. I think the future of this industry relies on being able to serve many customers at once and actually being able to find multi-tenant strategies in a world where most of the compute capacity is being bought by the largest labs and the largest hyperscalers. I think the way this happens is in a more managed platform, a more managed exchange. We see compute as a commodity. We see it being traded, bought, and sold by the biggest hyperscalers today in this way, and we see it becoming more granular over time. We differ from a Crusoe or an IREN in that we actually serve many of the smaller frontier labs and many of the smaller inference providers because we see their offtake as the future growth vector for this industry. Hey, my name is James Kullenstee, and I'm the CEO and co-founder of Alva Energy. Unlike every other speaker so far, we are not in the data center business, but we are in the business of providing megawatts so that Crusoe can sell megawatts. That is a really interesting distinction here. The data center world, the Neo Clouds, and even the hyperscalers talk about selling megawatts and building megawatts and gigawatts of capacity. Until very recently—and maybe there's some behind-the-meter stuff—they're not generating power. Some do, but most don't. We are forgetting right now how actually unique this moment is in the history of electric power load growth. In the United States right now, 50% of load growth is coming from data centers, and the other 50% is completely diffuse. It's building electrification and maybe some electric vehicles. It's all spread out. Right now, we are watching gigawatt load centers in single increments being added to the power grid in a way that hasn't structurally happened really since the founding of this industry 100 years ago by Thomas Alva Edison. Alva Energy was founded to develop technologies that allow a scalable energy source—nuclear energy—to actually be project financed on a gigawatt scale. We have developed a technology that allows us to operate existing nuclear power plants by about 200 to 300 megawatts electrical per reactor. Uniquely in the history of the nuclear power industry, we're able to do that in a project-financed way that is not relying on the utility rate base to raise that capital, but instead raising that capital off-balance sheet on a project-by-project basis. We're actually working with multiple pressurized water reactors right now in the United States to deploy this technology on a timeline of around three to five years. Although that sounds relatively slow, if you look at the timeline for a combined cycle gas turbine plant of similar size, it's actually the exact same timeline. The only difference is that this doesn't emit any carbon dioxide and is not constrained by natural gas or other geopolitical implications that have really implicated the fossil fuel markets both in the United States and here in Europe. Okay, thank you so much, amazing intro guys. Many follow-up questions. I want to start with Neo Clouds first. I think one of the biggest financing questions in Neo Cloud is: what about the mismatch between the data center, which is typically a 15-20 year asset, and the GPU, which has an asset life of five, six, or maybe seven years? However, Crusoe and IREN, you guys actually both procure your own energy. You buy your own data centers, so you do things a bit differently. I'd be curious to understand why you do that, how different it is relative to a traditional lease, and anything else relevant in your mind in terms of what drove your strategy in this direction. The reason we do the vertical integration is all about optimizing the reliability of the schedule for the customer. There are many stories in the industry right now of capacity not showing up on time, which has a lot of consequences downstream for the customer, whether they are an AI-native firm or a large corporation. We also do it to optimize the cost structure. Control your timing and control your margin is the "why" we are doing this. The translation in financing is that you use different vehicles for the different assets. You try to tie the financing—it's not one financing for the whole vertical cake—to the life cycle of each asset. We got some interesting news over the last year. Take H100 GPUs, which a couple of years ago, many would have predicted would have a very short lifetime. Well, effectively, in the current state of the market, the price per hour of a Hopper cluster is rising. So, the lifetime of that asset is longer than one may have thought a few years ago. Similarly on the energy side, we do a lot of energy scouting. We have about 40 gigawatts of pipeline and we currently manage 5 gigawatts of campuses. Sourcing is super important, but we also build energy behind the meter, or we build partnerships with people who are very creative there because that asset is also very capital intensive. I think it's about combining, but to do it well, ideally, you have some expertise in-house. I'm a software guy. I've built AI products at hyperscalers, and it's a delight to work with people who are experts in energy inside the company who can give me great feedback on the efforts being made on nuclear or have a strong opinion about gas turbines. Instead of relying only on partnerships between external entities, I like the vertical integration because we have the expertise in-house. We can debate these options, which each have different financing profiles. From our perspective, vertical integration gives us much better control over our scalability and de-risking of the delivery of these projects. What that does mean from a financing perspective is you end up with an interesting dynamic where you have GPUs with a much shorter useful life than the data center assets they're being housed in. One of the developments we're seeing is an increasing willingness and appetite for lenders to actually take some level of risk or attribute value to the post-contracted period that you have on your GPUs. To give a clearer example, I mentioned the Microsoft contracts; that is a five-year contract that is housed in our own data centers. Effectively, the first five years are funded through that contract. But post-that, we do not have a contracted revenue stream for the data center assets, which have a 20+ year useful life. What we're seeing within lending markets now is a realization that there is so much demand for these data center assets that even without a contracted revenue stream, lenders will look at the post-five-year period and attribute value. We're seeing some interesting structures where we are able to borrow significantly more against the data center assets than is actually funded through the contracted period. For a vertically integrated provider like us, this is going to open up very significant amounts of debt capital that haven't been available previously. And if I may quickly, on Erwan's earlier point, we at Orn track on-demand compute pricing. In the fall of 2025, we saw H100 pricing—and I know SemiAnalysis does this as well—at roughly $0.70. Today it is closer to $2.50. So we've seen this price increase over time. In regards to the residual value of GPUs—and being able to finance the end of life of a GPU that's being underwritten on a six-year straight-line depreciation schedule is difficult today. Finding a tenant from years four to seven or eight is not trivial. What we think is required in this industry is being able to find forward contracts on the day of financing. It goes to your point about finding those end-of-life contracts for the data center; the same sort of analogies exist for the GPU assets. Long-run, in terms of forward curves of compute pricing and residual values of hardware, this is something that can be solved with futures curves, which is what we're building at Orn. You anticipated my next question, which is about compute fungibility. It's becoming more and more important in the context of financing, which raises the question: today, how fungible is compute? How different is one asset to another? This drives differences in the way a futures index works. Tim, you are the one providing a solution to enable fungibility, so you probably have a good perspective. I talk to a lot of Neo Clouds. If you look at the at-scale version of success here, the best company in the world is Google in terms of a fully vertically integrated data center owner all the way to token seller. In the models just explained, in terms of how you can sell compute or watt-hours, fundamentally the best margin is selling a token. That is the currency of the ecosystem. Fundamentally, in the power law curves that exist, the vast majority of the largest consumers of compute are all the hyperscalers and the massive frontier clouds, because they are ultimately owning the token—they own the end developer, the end enterprise, or the end user. It is about how you are steering that token capacity and how you are trying to monetize it. Increasingly, we see all Neo Clouds wanting to be vertically integrated and move to selling the token. They don't want to sell the compute, actually. The margin profile is way better on selling the token. That's what everyone wants to do. If you look at the at-scale version of that, it's Google: they own the TPU, they own the software stack, they own their own cloud, and they distribute it. Both OpenAI and Anthropic are trying to do that, too, in different ways. Our belief is that the lock-in in the ecosystem is a software lock-in. Software was the most challenging component of the infrastructure stack. I make that a bold claim because getting power spun up, concrete, labor, water, and all the other components is very difficult, but the intrinsic lock-in that underlies the GPU economy is the CUDA ecosystem. If there is a software layer that gives more fungibility to the underlying compute, everything starts to change. Data center providers won't just go to one GPU provider; they will look at the economics across the transformer architecture. You already see this heterogeneous setup. AI went from running raw inference on a GPU to now massive agentic long-running workloads that require CPU orchestration. In the next 24 months, you will see increasing heterogeneity on the inference itself—disaggregation between pre-fill and decode. Memory prices have 12x'd in the last year because everyone is trying to figure out how to get better decode output to get a better dollar per token. That is exactly why they want to do it. Everyone wants to serve a token and they want to serve it more cheaply. If you serve it more cheaply, you get more scale, and if you get more scale, you have all the options in the world. Software is the one key thing that will radically change the underlying financing. To tie that back to financing, one of the dynamics we've seen is that if you're selling tokens on a short-term basis, it's harder to fund the upfront capital. Even if you look at the end users moving forward, they are going to be sub-investment grade entities. It is much harder to finance against those sub-investment grade entities if you're selling on these shorter-term, token-based models. But one of the interesting dynamics we're seeing on the financing side is a significantly increased willingness lately to fund against sub-investment grade counterparties. That opens up a huge range of end customers to us. Large-scale capital providers are willing to fund against smaller AI labs and enterprises. That opens up a lot of what you're talking about because if you can get the financing for the GPUs and data centers, and then service those parties on a shorter-term basis, you absolutely can increase your margins. Tokens are what the actual output is. We're spending a lot of time looking at how we can arrange financing to service the end-user demand we see coming through. I wanted to go back to energy. Erwan, you mentioned it is getting more capital-intensive. Crusoe has been at the forefront of buying gas turbine engines to power their data centers. James, how do you see the market on your end? Nuclear is special because of all the regulation. A few years ago, many expected a lot of nuclear PPAs, but we saw less than people expected overall. You are increasing capacity, which is a net asset to the grid. The issue on nuclear has not been demand; it has been supply. We have seen all major hyperscalers engage in very serious nuclear PPAs. In the case of Amazon, with the Talen deal, which was in some FERC middle, it has gotten much better for the Susquehanna nuclear plant, which was existing generation. What we are beginning to see is new generation brought online. First with Microsoft and bringing back Three Mile Island unit number one—buying 800 megawatts electrical plus for 15 years at about 116 dollars a megawatt hour chained to CPI. That is a huge PPA. We then saw Meta, with Vistra, buy about 440 megawatts of uprates from the Vistra fleet. We are seeing that nuclear is commanding a huge amount of attention from the major hyperscalers, at a very significant premium compared to what you can get on a renewable PPA or the wholesale markets. We are looking at how to solve the demand problem with more supply in three to five years. There aren't many more nuclear power plants turned off recently that can be turned on; they are already spoken for. Our solution is bringing up a much larger uprate market. We can deploy our technology into 41 reactors spread across the United States, almost all of which have existing transmission and interconnection headway. We don't have to build any new transmission or high-voltage switch gear. Unlike Vistra's uprates, which had to be financed at the corporate debt level, we can deploy our solution on a project-financeable basis. We can get firm fixed-price EPC contracts to do 100% of the scope. That makes it more attractive from debt and equity perspectives because the primary risk underwriters worry about is cost overruns. We can bond that with the EPCs themselves. It looks more to the capital markets like a combined cycle gas turbine or a solar project, even though the end product is an electric power source commanding a significant premium. Nuclear has an image that it is slow to bring online and extremely regulated. How do you navigate that? The uprates are a regulatory hack, if you will. The things that slow down new nuclear power plants are new licensing and new environmental impact statements. Because we're working at an existing licensed site, there are no mandatory hearings. We don't even see the regulatory process on our critical path; it is all procurement. We've bypassed all of the major regulatory slowdowns. This is a categorically different way to bring new nuclear capacity online than new builds. We anticipate new builds taking more of a decade-long timeline. We are working with partners to give them a continuous solution: power uprates on a three-to-five-year scale, and new builds on a five-to-ten-year scale. On the topic of financing, one of the biggest topics in the last year has been the increasing involvement of hyperscalers—and chip makers. We've seen Google strike first with over a gigawatt of capacity backstopped on the data center side. We've seen Broadcom backstop the TPU-as-a-service side, and Nvidia has announced something recently. All of these players are looking at every part of the stack. How do you see the involvement of chip makers in the space? Is it sustainable? In terms of being sustainable, you often hear critiques about it being a circular economy, just funding themselves. In reality, and it depends on each model, a lot of these backstops are interesting because the chip maker will stand behind them, but the backstop may never get hit. Is that really circular? You have actual underlying demand driving the consumption of the compute, and all you're doing is having a chip maker stand behind the initial funding from a credit perspective. I'm not sure that is circular in the way people describe it, but I think it is an interesting model where the chip maker allows the funding of the upfront capital, giving the owner of that compute the flexibility to service shorter-term contracts or put compute on demand knowing they have a financing backstop. Some of the criticisms are unfounded. I want to concur. We cannot lose sight of demand. It's not a mechanism that lives by itself out of context. If you look at the fleet of GPUs worldwide, the vast majority is consumed by long-term reservations. The next generation of Nvidia chips, Vera Rubin—it's very common to talk about five-year reservation contracts sold ahead of the chip showing up. What is that telling us? There is enormous demand, and despite these mechanisms, we're still far from being able to serve it. The supply chain pressure points are everywhere: memory, SSDs, GPUs. The megawatt GPU scarcity is translating into token scarcity. There is a lot of demand that is eventually the solution to all these financing loops. Kent said earlier there's enormous appetite to fund high-investment-grade offtake contracts like the Microsoft one. Is there a path to recreating the same appetite for tokens or short-term contracts if you build an index and exchange, and how close are we to that? A lot of the conversation has been about this timing mismatch. Supply is very long-term, but all the demand is happening right now. I need the compute tomorrow, I need the tokens today. Thinking about a forward curve or trading—there is going to be a lot of supply five or ten years out, selling the chips before deployment. But who is buying tokens 10 years out from today? What enterprise is saying they will pay hundreds of millions of dollars for tokens in 2032? It is hard to find those people. When we think about the market for tokens or compute, it follows a standard procedure. The foundation is here on the stage: everyone standing up the infrastructure that makes this trading possible. Tim, you guys have just been acquired by Qualcomm. Most of the GPU financing market goes to Nvidia or TPU-as-a-service, but mostly Nvidia. How do you think about bringing new types of chips online and creating a market for them, and how do you drive adoption? At the end of the day, any hyperscaler or Neo Cloud wants to get good utilization rates of the infrastructure they're selling. The thing not talked about is that most enterprises—unless they are like Anthropic—have enterprise utilization rates of core GPU infrastructure sitting at like 50 to 60%. Traffic is sinusoidal; you have spikes during the day and it falls off at night. People are trying to figure out how to increase utilization in the off-cycle to get a better TCO value. That is all software. Any hyperscaler or Neo Cloud looking at alternate compute wants to make sure that when they sell that to an enterprise, they can actually sell it. To sell it and get good utilization, you need good software. Most software is currently provided by the hardware vendor—Nvidia has CUDA, AMD has ROCm, Google has XLA. To an enterprise, the challenge is: why would I run different silicon if I have to use a different software stack constantly? We believe by having a unified model, it is much easier to move between different silicon, and that de-risks the overall investment thesis. That's what we are seeing in all the discussions we're having at a very large level. I have been gonged twice, so I will stop talking. All right. Thanks so much guys, thank you everyone for joining.

The Model-Agnostic AI Platform Betting That No Single Lab Will Win

Y Combinator(2.3M)·22:53·3.7K views·2026-07-23
Stanislas PoluCo-founder, Dust
TL;DRDust co-founder Stanislas Polu reflects on building an AI platform in the shadow of frontier labs, sharing hard-won lessons on model-agnostic architecture, lean fundraising, and navigating margin compression in the workplace AI market.
Background at Frontier Labs: Stanislas...

TL;DR

Dust co-founder Stanislas Polu reflects on building an AI platform in the shadow of frontier labs, sharing hard-won lessons on model-agnostic architecture, lean fundraising, and navigating margin compression in the workplace AI market.

From OpenAI to Founder: The Genesis of Dust

  • Background at Frontier Labs: Stanislas ("Stan") Polu spent five years at Stripe following an acquihire, then spent three years at OpenAI doing research-heavy work on LLMs and mathematics during the early breakthrough period.
  • Transition to Product: After treating his OpenAI tenure as the PhD he never completed, Stan wanted to return to product building rather than spending months scratching the surface on pure research with only fleeting moments of satisfaction.
  • Founding Dust (2002–2023): Launched alongside co-founder Gabriel to apply large language models to the workplace—a thesis that was considered a niche market at the time of their early fundraising.

The Evolution of Work and AI Product Strategy

  • The Disruption Thesis: Stan was deeply convinced that AI would radically disrupt work, a prediction that has proven true, particularly noting that software engineering work radically shifted over the past couple of months (since November 2025).
  • The Missing Plateau: Stan admits he was wrong in anticipating an early technological plateau; instead, the technology continues to accelerate, and work is actively being redefined across functions.
  • Horizontal vs. Vertical AI:
    • While early market consensus favored verticalized AI products due to simpler clean go-to-market motions, Dust chose a horizontal platform approach.
    • Product buckets are converging toward human-agent productivity suites, forcing startups to find differentiation through multi-player collaboration features.
  • Model-Agnostic Moat: Dust is strictly model-agnostic, comparing the alternative (locking customers into a single lab's tokens) to building a factory where machines only work with a single energy provider—akin to relying solely on Russian gas over French nuclear.

Fundraising, Valuation, and the European Context

  • Navigating the Funding Squeeze: Frontier labs absorb massive amounts of venture capital (e.g., investors preferring to put $1B into Anthropic rather than $100M across 10 startups), making the Series B fundraising environment complex.
  • The "Coffin Corner" of Over-Raising: Dust intentionally kept early rounds lean—raising a $5M seed when others were raising $100M–$200M for training—under the mantra No GPU Before PMF to avoid the dangerous trap of down-rounds.
  • Building in France: Choosing to build Dust in France adds a constraint of sovereignty and a slight friction from the US perspective, but Stan notes that when a company is performing well (like Spotify or Lovable), location becomes irrelevant to top-tier investors.
  • The Mistral Benchmark: Stan praises Mistral co-founder Arthur Mensch—who transitioned from a researcher with zero business background to leading a $12B European lab—as an impressive example of aggressive ambition.

Margins, Pricing, and the Threat of Labs

  • Pricing Transition: Dust operated on a flat, seat-based pricing model for three years to encourage usage, but soaring token consumption from longer agent loops compressed margins, forcing a transition to credit-based pricing.
  • Overcoming Lab Competition: Despite labs capturing margins at the token level and potentially undercutting application layers, historical precedents like Box, Dropbox, and 1Password prove that a well-crafted, differentiated product can successfully capture margin.
  • Open Source and Margin Pressure: Frontier lab margins sit at roughly 70% to 80% (deduced by comparing latency and pricing of models like GLM 5.2 on base-10/Fireworks against equivalent frontier models at a 9X price difference); open-source alternatives are expected to force a market recalibration.

If you raise too much and do not realize the potential of the money you raised, you end up in a coffin corner where the only outcome is a down-round. We raised our seed at a moment where it was fashionable to raise money for training—everybody was running a 100 million here, 200 million there—and we raised 5 million, which seems like silly. It made a ton of sense because the models were not that good, so you had to build a bit of scaffolding around that to make them useful. That value just disappears as the models get better. I think we are in a world where intelligence is commoditized, similar to the old good SaaS and internet days. If you're building a verticalized product in a given industry, we're going back to the advice we used to receive in 2010: find a network effect. If you don't have that, if it's just delivering intelligence to one and intelligence to another, and there is no network effect, the defensibility is changing. How do you think about margins? Margin is hard in these days. When you're a lab, you're capturing the margin at a token level, and then you build a product and you don't usually add any margin on top of this. So for you, how do you think about charging? We used to have a seat-based pricing which made sense for three years, which has become difficult because we wanted to favor usage and value creation. Obviously, over the past six months, the usage has exploded, the agent loops are getting longer, and models are just using more tokens all over the place. Our margins have compressed and we are in the middle of transitioning to a credit-based pricing. Today, if you are building at the product layer, there is no way around credit-based pricing because people want to use more AI and they'll just max use as much as you give them. That is the only way to maintain your margins. There are precedents where companies managed to sell a product on top of an infrastructure that captures the margins and still built a successful business, like Box, Dropbox, or 1Password. If you look at the latency of models, you can compare the cost of a model; the difference between a frontier model and an equivalent model today is like 9X. So there is a very good chance that those labs are creating margins of something like 70 or 80%. Hopefully, with open source coming up, they'll recalibrate the market. The Philosopher CEO | Clay Co-Founder Kareem Amin

Sequoia Capital(233.0K)·69:27·3.1K views·2026-07-23
Kareem AminCo-founder, Clay
TL;DR"A lot of people think you should build from a place of lack, or like, you have a chip on your shoulder, you need to prove…
Kareem Amin, co-founder and CEO of Clay, built a breakout B2B tech success by operating from a philosophy of wholeness rather than lack, committing to power and flexibility over oversimplification, and pioneering the "go-to-market engineer" category. Rejection of the...

TL;DR: Kareem Amin, co-founder and CEO of Clay, built a breakout B2B tech success by operating from a philosophy of wholeness rather than lack, committing to power and flexibility over oversimplification, and pioneering the "go-to-market engineer" category.

1. Building from Wholeness and the Present Moment

  • Rejection of the "Lack" Narrative: Kareem Amin argues that while building from a chip on your shoulder or a place of lack works, it causes immense collateral damage; instead, he chooses to build from "wholeness" where he has nothing to prove.
  • The Reality Commitment: Amin views his top commitment as reality itself, noting that life constantly provides feedback and that founders waste energy trying to escape the present.
  • Presence vs. Future Anxiety: Drawing on a silent retreat experience, Amin realized that constantly living in the future accelerates one's psychological timeline toward death; true clarity comes from embracing the present and even being grateful for boredom.

2. The Five-Year Desert and the Power of Commitment

  • The Long, Strange Trip: Clay spent roughly five years in a meandering phase—two years building, two years confused, and then a pivot toward absolute commitment.
  • Overcoming the Trap of the "Biggest Thing": Early on, Amin and co-founder Nikolai kept shifting their vision to pursue the biggest possible venture, falling into the trap of changing markets instead of focusing on what was directly in front of them.
  • Ignoring Customers Early On: Amin believes the common advice to "focus entirely on the customer" is flawed in a startup's early days; for the first seven years, Amin blocked out customer requests and built what he wanted as the primary user before pivoting to a customer-centric model.

3. Flipping Market Assumptions: The Clay Playbook

  • Core Architectural Decisions: Clay deliberately flipped every conventional assumption in its market by opting to:
    • Sell to technical users rather than traditional sales reps.
    • Charge for usage and compute rather than per-seat licensing.
    • Prioritize power and flexibility over out-of-the-box simplicity.
    • Act as a data marketplace rather than owning the data itself.
  • Category Creation via "Go-To-Market Engineering": Rather than forcing a term, Amin and his team named a wave already happening by calling their own customer-facing staff "go-to-market engineers," then empowering agencies to adopt the title and letting the market organically broaden the category.
  • Handling Pricing Backlash with Radical Transparency: When Clay introduced a usage-based pricing split between compute and data that generated pushback, Amin relied on publishing word-for-word internal memos to prove that transparency equals trust.

4. Unconventional Operating Style and CEO Philosophy

  • The Momentum Detective: As CEO, Amin describes his primary operational mode as finding and fixing areas of the company where momentum is waning rather than hovering over hot zones.
  • Abolishing Traditional Structures: Clay operates with just-in-time decision-making, has no standing executive team meetings, and has eliminated recurring weekly one-on-one meetings for roles like engineering.
  • Devotion Over Discipline: Amin rejects cookie-cutter CEO playbooks (such as "hire slow, fire fast" or aggressive 996 work culture) in favor of building environments driven by authentic devotion, high standards paired with deep forgiveness, and embracing intellectual ambiguity.

Notable Quotes

"A lot of people think you should build from a place of lack, or like, you have a chip on your shoulder, you need to prove something... and to me, I've decided to create from a place of wholeness, so I'm like, I don't need anything, I'm not trying to prove anything." — Kareem Amin
"When I decided to commit, I stopped listening to everybody. I was like, I'm not listening to you, I'm not listening to my customers, by the way, who are wrong about what they want or how. If you listen to the wrong people at the time when it's like a small idea, you kill it." — Kareem Amin
"Transparency equals trust. It's not even correlated with it, it's like equals it. It equals trust." — Kareem Amin

AI for Chip Design & Chip Design for AI | Ricursive Intelligence | RAISE Summit 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·16:53·17 views·2026-07-23
Anna GoldieFounder & CEO, Ricursive Intelligence Azalia MirhoseiniCo-Founder & CTO, Ricursive Intelligence
TL;DRRicursive Intelligence is automating end-to-end chip design using a dual-loop AI architecture—combining proprietary inner-loop optimization tools with outer-loop LLMs—to accelerate development and usher in a "designless era" of workload-tailored custom silicon.
Founding Team &...

TL;DR

Ricursive Intelligence is automating end-to-end chip design using a dual-loop AI architecture—combining proprietary inner-loop optimization tools with outer-loop LLMs—to accelerate development and usher in a "designless era" of workload-tailored custom silicon.

Company Background & Foundations

  • Founding Team & Origins: Founded by Anna Goldie (CEO) and Azalia Mirhoseini (CTO), who met on their first day at Google Brain in 2016 and later led the Machine Learning for Systems team.
  • The AlphaChip Precursor: Created AlphaChip, a deep reinforcement learning agent generating superhuman chip layouts in hours instead of weeks/months.
  • Industry Deployment: Deployed across Google's last four generations of TPUs, Axion CPUs, Pixel phones, Waymo chips, and externally by MediaTek.

Three-Phase Company Roadmap

  • Phase One (Current): Accelerate traditional chip design from 1+ years to drastically shorter timeframes by overhauling physical design and verification for chipmakers like Nvidia, AMD, and Intel.
  • Phase Two (Democratization / "Designless Era"): Enable application and frontier AI labs (such as those building models like Claude) to bypass the traditional hardware lottery by using Ricursive as a platform to co-design computer architectures tailored to specific new workloads.
  • Phase Three (Recursive Self-Improvement): Build proprietary chips, train proprietary models, co-evolve them, and close the recursive self-improving loop.

Technical Approach: The Dual-Loop Architecture

  • Dual-Loop Optimization:
    • *Inner Loop:* Proprietary optimization tools (like a static timing analysis engine) running 100x to 1,000x faster than traditional commercial tools with 0.999+ correlation to actual tools, executing in seconds or milliseconds.
    • *Outer Loop:* An LLM that leverages the high-speed inner loop to perform agentic optimization, tuning parameters, and even rewriting tools to optimize metrics such as wire length.
  • End-to-End Scope: Automating the entire workflow from architectural design and exploration down to GDS2 (the format sent to fabs like TSMC).
  • Customization Impact: Focusing on high-consequence microarchitecture decisions—such as systolic array dimensions and memory hierarchy organization—which yield multiplier performance improvements and Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) gains compared to downstream physical layout optimization (which yields ~20% power improvement).

Team Composition & Expertise

  • Multidisciplinary Talent: The team includes engineers who worked on frontier AI models (Claude, Gemini, Grok) alongside silicon experts who built iPhone chips and Google TPUs.
  • Academic Ties: Co-founder Azalia Mirhoseini serves as a faculty member at Stanford University, where related research on AI compilers (replacing compiler stacks like PyTorch-to-PTX) has been conducted.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Custom Chip Proliferation: The scale of AI deployment across robotics, self-driving cars, AR/VR, and space exploration will drive massive growth in custom chips tailored to diverse AI form factors — *Azalia Mirhoseini & Anna Goldie*
  • The Designless Era: Application companies will increasingly skip building massive in-house teams of hundreds or thousands of chip design experts, instead outsourcing custom silicon creation to platforms like Ricursive — *Azalia Mirhoseini*

Fast, Efficient Inference at Scale with Heterogeneous Hardware | Gimlet Labs x d-Matrix | RAISE 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·18:13·25 views·2026-07-23
Zain AsgarCo-Founder & CEO, Gimlet Labs Sid ShethCEO, d-Matrix
TL;DR1.
TL;DR: Gimlet Labs is building the industry's first multi-silicon inferencing cloud to decompose AI workloads and allocate them across heterogeneous hardware, achieving 4x to 5x gains in both capacity and interactive performance. Core Value Proposition: Gimlet Labs aims to...

1. TL;DR: Gimlet Labs is building the industry's first multi-silicon inferencing cloud to decompose AI workloads and allocate them across heterogeneous hardware, achieving 4x to 5x gains in both capacity and interactive performance.

Company Vision & Approach

  • Core Value Proposition: Gimlet Labs aims to make AI inference workloads approximately 10x more efficient by decomposing them into granular components, individual models, and layers, and routing them to the optimal hardware type.
  • The Multi-Silicon Strategy: Rather than routing between homogeneous deployments (like NVIDIA vs. AMD), Gimlet leverages the distinct strengths of various architectures—including CPUs, GPUs, and neo-accelerators like d-Matrix, Cerebras, and LPX.
  • Analogy for Heterogeneous Computing: Zain Asgar compares the approach to choosing the right vehicle for the job: *"If you had like a Ferrari and like a minivan... you probably would not necessarily want to drive your Ferrari to the grocery store to go grocery shopping."*

Technical Tradeoffs & Architecture

  • Hardware Constraints: Most hardware forces tradeoffs between compute (scaling quadratically with chip area) and memory bandwidth (scaling linearly via the chip's perimeter).
  • Balancing Workloads:
    • *GPUs:* Typically feature large external memory with relatively low bandwidth.
    • *SRAM-Centric Chips (d-Matrix, Cerebras, LPX):* Offer high memory bandwidth but are limited by single-chip capacity (though d-Matrix addresses this by stacking memory directly on top of compute).
  • Full-Stack Challenges: Operating a heterogeneous cloud requires solving compatibility issues across distinct programming models (spatial vs. GPU architectures), non-NVLink networking topologies (relying on Ethernet), and data center electrical and cooling systems.

Market Context & Target Applications

  • Industry Drivers: A severe scarcity of data center resources combined with the rise of agentic workloads (which introduce diverse CPU and tool-call tasks) created the impetus for heterogeneous scaling.
  • Key Use Cases: Primary customer pull is focused on coding agents, where delivering instantaneous, low-latency responses enables developers to iterate as fast as they can think.
  • Performance Metrics: Gimlet's approach yields approximately a 4x to 5x increase in capacity for the same cost, and a 4x to 5x improvement in interactive performance for end users.

Founder Background & Execution

  • Company Status: Gimlet Labs has raised close to $80 million in a Series A round led by Menlo Ventures.
  • Leadership Team: Co-Founder & CEO Zain Asgar is a repeat entrepreneur (previously founded Pixie Labs, acquired by New Relic), former Stanford computer science professor, NVIDIA architect, Google engineering lead, and Benchmark Capital entrepreneur-in-residence. Interviewed by Sid Sheth, Co-Founder & CEO of d-Matrix.
  • Scaling Realities: Because true heterogeneous hardware systems do not yet exist commercially, Gimlet has been forced to become vertically integrated, building and managing its own production data center capacity.
  • Entrepreneurial Learnings: Asgar's key takeaway from his first startup is to make high-value, high-conviction strategic bets early rather than constantly negotiating middle-ground compromises.

Notable Quotes

*"We take a workload, decompose it in the granular components, and even like individual models and even layers within an individual model and run it on different types of hardware."* — Zain Asgar

>

So Gimlet always had this goal of basically making inference workloads about 10 times more efficient, and we have a pretty unique way of doing that and unique doesn't always necessarily mean good, but we think our approach is pretty interesting and actually scalable for the industry. We started realizing that in a lot of AI inferencing workloads, there's a fair amount of diversity and this actually increases with agentic workloads, where you have tool calls, other things happening, and CPU workloads happening. We basically built a system where we can absorb these workloads, partition them into small fine-grain components, and then allocate these different components to different types of hardware. We usually say it's multi-silicon because we typically allocate the work to different types of chips. Some of it might be CPU workloads, some of them might be on GPU, some of them might be on neo-accelerators like d-Matrix, or Cerebras, or even something like an LPX, where we can utilize the differences between the hardware to drive best-in-class performance. That's really the unique value proposition of Gimlet; we're truly multi-silicon. We don't just run on things like here's an NVIDIA deployment and here's an AMD deployment, route between those two; we take a workload, decompose it in the granular components, and even individual models and even layers within an individual model and run it on different types of hardware. One intuition for this is that if you had a Ferrari and a minivan, you probably would not necessarily want to drive your Ferrari to the grocery store to go grocery shopping, but it does have use cases, so you want to try to figure out when you need the performance and when you don't, and try to allocate the right workload to the right hardware. So Zain, maybe tell us a little bit about what you guys started Gimlet for. There were things happening in the industry at the time, especially in 2025, that makes this very relevant for what you guys are doing today. What was the impetus? So when we originally started Gimlet, we were actually thinking about how we can even put workloads on very heterogeneous hardware, like, how do we actually put models on laptops and cell phones and really distribute things out. So we got particularly good at figuring out how to distribute workloads to different target hardware, especially hardware that's not very homogeneous and doesn't completely look like a GPU. There are kind of a couple of different trends that push this into scaling this out on the data center side. I think one of the things is there's this pretty strong scarcity of resources on the data center side and we actually increase not only the available types of chips we can use, but also improve the efficiency. You get this double effect where not only can we use more silicon, but we actually use the silicon more efficiently, so you ultimately get a lot more capacity. Based on our blogs where we talk a lot about these results, we can usually push the capacity for the same cost by about 4X or 5X, and we can actually push the performance which is basically how interactive it is for a specific user by about a similar amount of 4 to 5X. And any specific applications that come to mind? Where are you seeing pull from your end customers? What kind of applications are they trying to accelerate to take advantage of this? Yeah. So the main use cases we're targeting are actually for coding agents, because that's where people are in the loop. Delivering a faster experience is an order of magnitude change for just people's development workflows. I'm an engineer myself and sometimes I notice—this is actually not true, but when I'm using some coding agent, I'm like, I could literally type out this code faster because I'm seeing it generate the code and it's actually a very annoying experience. If everything was instant, you can kind of work as fast as you can think through what the implications are and what you're trying to do and just iterate a lot faster. For me, I fundamentally view coding agents as a tool where I want to experiment with new ideas as quickly as possible and that's actually an area where we think that pushing performance while maintaining a reasonable efficiency is actually pretty game-changing for the industry. Is the plan at some point to actually have a small installation of Gimlet orchestrated multi-silicon hardware and then you can actually use coding on top of it and have the Gimlet team actually use it and try it out? Yeah, so we use frontier models for a lot of our coding stuff, but some of our more proprietary things, we actually run our own coding agents and models internally because we can't use any other frontier ones and we run it on top of our own stack. That's excellent. The other thing is obviously hardware is hot again. There are numerous different types of startups that are all building AI chips with different strengths and emphasis on different areas. How do you decide who to partner with based on the types of problems that you guys are trying to solve and what is the best way for an AI chip company to partner with Gimlet? Yeah. To answer that question, maybe it's important to dive in a little bit more on the technical details and understand what we're trying to optimize. Realistically, what we're trying to do is at a high level, when you take a look at most hardware, there are different types of tradeoffs you can make. You can make a tradeoff between how much compute you have on a chip, you can trade off how much memory bandwidth you have available—and this could be memory bandwidth to external memory or even memory bandwidth to internal memory—and how much networking you have available and what the memory capacity is. Ultimately, you can say that there's a cost for dollars per gigaflop or teraflop, or dollars for gigabyte per second of memory bandwidth. If you take a look at most traditional architectures, they pretty much bring in all the memory through the chip's external interface. If you think about a particular chip and how the memory comes in, what typically happens is that the compute will scale as the area, so it will be the side squared, it will scale pretty rapidly, whereas the memory will not scale very efficiently because it's coming in through the perimeter. The perimeter is linear and area is quadratic; those things are going to get further and further apart as chips get bigger. What we fundamentally do is actually try to figure out how to use that tradeoff across different types of architectures to balance memory-bound versus compute-bound workloads. If you think about a spectrum, something like a GPU typically has a lot of external memory, but the bandwidth is relatively low. Something like more of an SRAM-centric chip, like d-Matrix or Cerebras or LPX, they have a lot of memory bandwidth, but it's usually pretty limited by how much they can fit on a single chip. It creates this tradeoff where we have to really understand how to allocate the workloads to different pieces of hardware so we can utilize the high memory bandwidth without losing the efficiency of compute. Gimlet basically takes these workloads, splits them up, and runs them on different pieces of target hardware so that we can get the maximum efficiency. Yeah, I think that memory bandwidth piece obviously is super critical. I was in my fireside chat just about less than an hour back, and we talked exactly about what are the different options we have available to work on that. At d-Matrix, we're taking this approach where we stack memory directly on top of compute, so instead of relying on the perimeter of the chip, we kind of rely on the surface area of the chip. The issue with SRAM memory is obviously capacity, so how do we expose more area available to memory? By stacking memory on top of compute, obviously, we can pack more and address the capacity problem that SRAM chips suffer from. So hopefully, we'll come to you someday with a chip that has unlimited capacity and unlimited bandwidth, and you can then help us orchestrate that. I mean, that's part of the reason we're interested in some of the d-Matrix chips, because stacking the memory will solve not only the bandwidth issue, but also the capacity issue. Maybe talk about the approaches. This is not an easy problem; you're breaking new ground here, literally. Heterogeneous compute is something the whole world got interested in maybe six or eight months ago. We had the cloud code moment end of last year, we had OpenCL, and we truly entered the agentic era, call it early 2026. There's a lot of talk about orchestrating different types of hardware, but this is not easy because every chip company has its own software stack. How do you put this all together, run a workload, and partition it? There's just a lot of nuances. Maybe if you can help the audience understand the challenges involved in getting this done, that'd be great. Yeah, and I think I'll probably start by saying that I don't actually think this is a pure software problem either. Part of working with heterogeneous hardware, and it's part of the reason we became much more of a vertically integrated NeoCloud, is that there isn't actually anybody deploying true heterogeneous hardware today. The problem actually starts all the way from different software stacks; every chip company has its own way of programming their chip, especially with neo-accelerators. The programming models that have worked in the past just don't really work anymore. There's lots of nuance to why that is, but typically, most of these stacked or SRAM-type chips are some form of spatial architectures and the programming model used for GPUs just doesn't really translate well. You start with that issue, and then you keep going down; then you have to deal with the fact that the networking is usually not compatible. You can't really use NVLink across these chips, although I know d-Matrix is working on solving some of that. So you have to rely on Ethernet and other open technologies where you can easily connect these things together. If you want to do any kind of true disaggregation, you're pretty sensitive to network latencies, so you have to build up all the topologies and stuff so that it actually works. You move down to the actual deployment in data centers, and typically, people don't usually deploy different types of racks together. You might run into issues with how do you deal with different cooling systems, different electrical systems, so it's really a full-stack problem that you need to solve, starting all the way at the software and ending at how do you distribute cooling and electricity. And is the vision, do you see Gimlet building their own data centers at some point to go full-stack? Yeah, we already are, because we're kind of forced to do it since nobody actually has any of these systems. So we've already started building and managing a fair amount of production data center capacity. You're a repeat entrepreneur. You did a software company first, observability tools for Kubernetes, that got acquired. This is the second time you're doing this. What are some of the big learnings for you from the first company that you're taking into the second company? What excites you about this company, this time, versus doing the previous one? You also start working with other people on your team, and you start negotiating things about where exactly you want to land. One of the learnings for me is that for startups, it's much better to commit to a direction and at least run with that for a little bit, rather than trying to meander and do the middle-ground non-stop. It's actually very easy to get stuck in that trap because as humans we want to make everybody happy, so you always try to negotiate middle grounds—at least that's how I operate. But one of the things I learned is that you want to make high-conviction decisions and then late-bind the things that don't matter as much. With Gimlet, we've been doing a lot more like, "we really believe in this and we need to scale this quickly." The thing that really excites me about Gimlet is that I think we're in the position, especially with the customers and stuff that we work with, where we can redefine how data centers of the future will get built for inference. So far everyone kind of does the same thing: we deploy large fleets of GPUs, bigger and bigger fleets for bigger models and more capacity. I think we're pretty unique where we're starting to reconsider that scaling motion and starting to think about how we actually use new types of hardware and new types of chips to build much more of an inference-centric system. Well, fascinating, just fascinating. We are very lucky to be partners with you. Thank you for the partnership. Any closing comments before we end here? Sovereign Stacks: Building Trusted AI on National Terms | RAISE Summit 2026

RAISE Summit(1.8K)·36:08·8 views·2026-07-23
Mohsen MoazamiVice Chair, DDN Thomas Di GiacomoCTPO, SUSE Cory HawkveltCPTO, Hyperstack
TL;DRSovereignty in AI is about maintaining practical control, choice, and a "Plan B" across the infrastructure stack rather than pursuing complete isolation or relying purely on regulatory compliance checkboxes.
Addressing these challenges requires balancing massive infrastructure...

TL;DR

Sovereignty in AI is about maintaining practical control, choice, and a "Plan B" across the infrastructure stack rather than pursuing complete isolation or relying purely on regulatory compliance checkboxes. Addressing these challenges requires balancing massive infrastructure scale (such as $100 billion compute demands) with open ecosystems and pragmatic business realities.

Defining Sovereign AI: Control vs. Isolation

  • Pragmatic Goals over Paperwork: Gleb Budman (Backblaze) noted that sovereignty is frequently confused with regulatory paperwork or mere residency, arguing that true sovereignty is the pragmatic ability for an organization to manage, move, and access its data and workflows without punitive taxes like egress fees.
  • Control, Not Autarchy: Mohsen Moazami (DDN / ex-Groq) emphasized that sovereignty is about control and avoiding single-vendor hostage situations, not isolation, pointing out that Saudi Arabia achieved sovereign compute stack control while successfully exporting tokens.
  • Vendor Independence: Thomas Di Giacomo (SUSE) defined sovereignty as the freedom to exit, pivot, and maintain choice, pointing to open-source software as a critical mandate supported by the European Commission to prevent vendor lock-in.
  • Business Enabler: Simon Muskett (Digital Realty) described infrastructure platforms as the foundational enabler of sovereign decisions, where data location, security, and network architecture directly dictate business outcomes.

Navigating the AI Stack and Infrastructure Realities

  • Cross-Stack Interdependence: Simon Muskett highlighted that all layers of the stack—from data centers and cooling (such as Digital Realty's Paris partnership with Mistral) to networking and models—are equally critical, requiring holistic design rather than focusing solely on frontier models.
  • Risk Mitigation and Plan B: Thomas Di Giacomo argued that infrastructure layers (like hardware and cloud foundations) require a dual-vendor or backup strategy because failure at this layer halts mission-critical business operations.
  • Granular Enterprise Use Cases: Corey Hawkvelt (Hyperstack) explained that enterprise AI sovereignty is not "carte blanche"; an enterprise may route low-sensitivity tasks (like marketing cat memes) to the cheapest global provider while keeping sensitive finance data under strict local compliance.
  • Global Scale Disconnect: Mohsen Moazami contrasted European neo-clouds operating at scales of 10,000 GPUs due to 3-year depreciation risks with American model companies handling single-customer purchase orders worth $100 billion in compute.
  • Tokenomics and Efficiency: Simon Muskett noted that tokenomics, latency, and liquid cooling requirements—costing hundreds of millions or billions of dollars—drive modern GPU placement and infrastructure profitability.

Geopolitical Pressures and Innovation Trade-Offs

  • Engineers Under Constraints: Corey Hawkvelt argued that US foreign policy and export controls act as a positive catalyst for business and engineering innovation, forcing nations like China to build competitive indigenous models and hardware (such as Huawei GPUs).
  • The Rise of "Buy Europe": Thomas Di Giacomo observed that while Europe has historically lagged and been shy about national procurement preferences, a "buy Europe" mindset is emerging, which can successfully revitalize European technology sectors.
  • Interdependence as a Pre-requisite: Gleb Budman compared data infrastructure to airports: *“The only reason that any of those are valuable is because the planes fly between them,”* warning that hyper-restricted data residency without cross-border mobility renders data useless.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Obsolescence of the Term: Thomas Di Giacomo predicts that by 2030 or 2035, the specific word "sovereignty" will no longer be discussed because the underlying concepts of control and independence will have matured.

Notable Quotes

*"Sovereignty is not about isolation, it's about control... there is no equivalent of NVIDIA anywhere on the planet."* — Mohsen Moazami
*"That sovereignty is residency. I mean, fundamentally, just because you put some... just because you put the airport here, if the planes don't fly anywhere, you have nothing."* — Gleb Budman
*"Sovereignty cannot be enforced by regulations. The business and technology will drive the adoption."* — Thomas Di Giacomo

And then right next to me is Thomas Di Giacomo, Chief Technology and Product Officer for Suse. Suse, Suse. Sorry. All right. So, sovereignty is perhaps one of the hottest political topics in Europe at the moment. It's long been part of the French political vocabulary certainly. But I think, post the entropic moment a few weeks ago, it's become a major priority all across Europe. I think every nation has woken up, even in the last couple years, to the fact that economic dependencies can be weaponized by other nations to their own interest. And I think for Europe especially, for a region that has built its recent economic history on the notion that capitalism and the rule of law were here to stay, it's a major shift in the way that we consider our economy and especially our relationship to technology. Yet, I think sometimes the calls for sovereignty are somewhat naive. Some seem to equate them with the idea of complete autarchy or complete separation from other nations, and I think my panel will agree that's probably not the right way to think about it. So the idea with this panel is to explore sovereignty as a practical infrastructure and business question, and not only as a political slogan. So the key question for us is, what does it mean to build trusted AI infrastructure where no country or company can fully control any part of the stack? It means different things to governments, to customers, to companies, and so to get us started on that thought, I would like to hand over to Gleb to share his thoughts on what we mean when we talk about sovereignty in AI. And so I think the question for all of us to wrestle with is, what do we want? What's our goal when we say sovereignty? And I think that's going to depend and differ, whether it's a government, a company, an institution, even, you know, when we talk about Europe, right? Europe is not a singular entity; Europe is a combination of a number of countries, and even within certain countries, different organizations are structured differently. So we really have to struggle with what is the practical goal of sovereignty. And I think sometimes sovereignty gets confused with residency, or sometimes with security. But I think when I think of sovereignty, it's: do I, as the organization responsible for whatever I'm responsible for, can I do what I want with my data, my workflows, my initiatives, right? And so that can mean it has to be in a certain place, that can mean I have to be able to use it in certain ways, that can mean I have to protect it.
